"Nate." He turns fully now, knees cracking on the concrete floor. "How are you doing this?"

And here it is. The moment I've been manufacturing for months. I need Marcus to trust my predictions because eventually — not now, not soon, but eventually — I'm going to need people who believe me when I say something impossible. Something about a signal. Something about the sky.

"I just pay attention," I say. "Like, really pay attention. Everyone's so busy reacting that they don't look at where things are going."

Marcus stares at me for a long beat. Then he grins, gap-toothed and wide. "So what else? What about the Tigers? We going to State?"

"Marcus. I'm not a magic eight ball."

"Come on."

"I genuinely do not remember who wins Iowa high school baseball this year," I say, and this is completely honest, and he takes it as a joke, and we move on. But something has shifted. There's a new weight behind his eyes when he looks at me. Like I'm someone to be listened to.

Which is what I wanted. And it makes me sick.

---

April. Mr. Kowalski's AP Government class, last period, that dead zone of the afternoon when everyone's thinking about practice or dinner or nothing at all. He's talking about pandemic preparedness — specifically, how underfunded it is, how we learned nothing from SARS. It's a sidebar, barely connected to whatever we're actually supposed to be covering about executive power.

I raise my hand. "There's going to be a pandemic soon."

Kowalski adjusts his glasses. "That's... an interesting contribution, Nate."

"Not like, tomorrow. But we're overdue. Influenza mutation rates, factory farming concentration, international air travel volume—" Again with the borrowed language, the armchair epidemiologist act. "Something like swine flu, maybe. In the next few months."

A few people laugh. Derek, two rows back, doesn't laugh. He's watching me with that measuring look he has, like he's pricing something.

"Well," Kowalski says. "Statistically, you're not wrong. We are overdue for a novel strain. But that's like saying we're overdue for an earthquake. It doesn't mean—"

"Sure," I say. "Just thought it was relevant."

I sit back. It's April 7th. The first WHO advisories about H1N1 will hit the news in about two weeks. By the end of the month, the cable channels will be running PANDEMIC FEARS? chyrons twenty-four hours a day.

And I'll have called it.

Anyway. The point is—

---

Three weeks later, Marcus texts me. It's a Tuesday evening and I'm at the kitchen table pretending to study for AP Calc while actually reading a library book about disaster communication theory. My silver Samsung buzzes against the wood.

**dude. swine flu. u called it.**

I stare at the screen for a while. Then I type back: **just paying attention man**

**ur like a prophet or something lol**

**lol** I type, and set the phone face-down, and look at the ceiling, and think about Cassandra again. She screamed and tore her hair and nobody listened. I murmur and shrug and everyone leans in. Which version of the curse is worse?

From the living room, the TV is running coverage. CDC CONFIRMS NOVEL H1N1 STRAIN. I can hear Diane saying something to Frank about whether they should be worried. Frank's response is a grunt that means *probably not but I'll google it later.*

"Nate?" Sophie appears in the kitchen doorway. She's holding a bowl of cereal — her standard evening snack, Lucky Charms with the marshmallows picked out first. The braces make her look younger than thirteen, which is already young. She's watching me with the bowl held in both hands like a chalice.

"What's up, Soph?"

"You told Mom two weeks ago to buy extra canned soup."

I did say that. Casually. In passing. "I said it might be good to have some extra stuff around. Because of the economy. People are losing jobs—"

"And now there's a flu pandemic."

"It's not a pandemic yet. And the soup thing was about—"

"You're not you anymore."

She says it flat. No accusation, no drama. A thirteen-year-old stating what she observes, the way she might say *the kitchen smells like gas* or *there's a spider on your back.* Simple reporting of an obvious and vaguely dangerous fact.

"Soph. People change. I'm just—"

"People don't change." She takes a bite of cereal, chews, swallows. "They just get better at hiding."

She walks back to the living room. I sit there with my disaster communication book and my buzzing phone and the weight of being seen by someone too young to dismiss and too smart to fool.

She is thirteen and she is right. I'm not me anymore. The person she knew — the version of her brother who ignored her and drank cheap vodka in parking lots and couldn't be bothered to remember what books she liked — that person is gone. Dissolved, like everything else, in the gray light of a June afternoon seventeen years from now. What's left is something wearing his face. Something that knows too much and can't say why.

I close the textbook. Open it again. The numbers don't mean anything. They never did.

---

The college essay writes itself. Not literally — I spend three days at the library and go through six drafts — but the ideas pour out with the conviction of someone who knows exactly what's coming.

*Community resilience in the face of cascading systems failure.* That's my angle. UNI's emergency management program is small, underfunded, the kind of thing practical Iowans study when they're thinking about tornado response and flood logistics. My essay talks about interconnection — how a financial collapse weakens the social fabric that disaster response depends on, how isolation kills more people than the disaster itself, how the communities that survive are the ones that built trust before they needed it.

It's genuine. Every word. I believe what I'm writing because I lived the alternative — the world where nobody prepared, where the bonds between people had been corroding for decades before the sky changed and the aliens came and turned everything they touched into gray sludge. I watched neighbors turn on each other in hours. I watched people die alone in apartments because nobody knew their names.

So the essay is real. But it's also strategy. Emergency management gives me a framework. Access to communication infrastructure. A reason to be thinking about disaster scenarios without looking insane.

I can't tell the difference anymore between the genuine part and the strategic part. Maybe there isn't one. Maybe that's what it means to be whoever I am now.

Frank reads the essay one evening without being asked — I leave it on the counter, an invitation, and he picks it up while his coffee cools. He reads slow, lips not moving but eyes tracking with visible effort. When he finishes he sets it down and nods once.

"Good," he says. "That's good, bud."

Three months later I'll get the acceptance letter. But Frank's nod is the moment I know it worked.

---

May collapses into June. Senior year ends not with a bang but with a series of progressively meaningless obligations — final exams I could pass in my sleep, a senior prank involving plastic flamingos that I participate in because participation is a form of camouflage, and the slow gathering weight of graduation.

GM files for bankruptcy on June 1st. Chapter 11. Largest industrial bankruptcy in American history. Marcus calls me that night and says nothing for about ten seconds.

"Nate."

"Yeah."

"June. You said June."

"I said probably June."

"Nate, what the fuck."

I laugh, and it sounds wrong even to me. "I read a lot of financial stuff, man. It's not magic. The signs were—"

"The *election.* The *flu.* And now—"

"Marcus. I'm just observant. Some people are good at patterns."

He's quiet again. I can hear him breathing, the faint sound of his garage radio in the background. Then: "Okay. But if you ever, like... see something bad coming. For me, or for people. You'd say something, right?"

My throat closes. "Yeah," I manage. "I'd say something."

"Okay." A pause. "Okay, cool. See you tomorrow? Graduation rehearsal?"

"Yeah. See you tomorrow."

I hang up and sit on the edge of my bed and look at my hands. These seventeen-year-old hands that haven't built anything or broken anything yet. I think about Marcus at thirty-five, in the timeline I came from — I don't know what happened to him. After the invasion, I don't know what happened to anyone. The last time I saw a human face before waking up here was a woman I didn't know, running, and then not running anymore.

I'd say something. Yeah. I'm going to say something. I'm going to build a network of people who trust me enough to listen when the time comes, and Marcus is going to be the first one, and maybe — maybe — he won't be running toward gray light in 2026.

Maybe none of them will.

---

Graduation day. June 6th. The sun is out, which feels like a personal affront because I'm wearing a black polyester gown over khakis and I'm sweating through both layers before we even line up.

The gym is packed. Folding chairs in rows, families crammed together, someone's baby crying in the back with that thin desperate sound that means they've been crying for a while. The Class of 2009 files in to "Pomp and Circumstance" played by a brass section that's missing its first-chair trumpet, and we sit in alphabetical order, which means I'm between Brianna Caldwell and Thomas Cho, neither of whom I will ever see again after today.

Derek gives the commencement speech. Of course he does. Class president, 4.0 GPA, captain of debate team — the full resumé of a kid who figured out early that performance is power. He stands at the podium in his pressed gown with the gold honor cords and he talks about *seizing opportunity in uncertain times.* About how this class — our class — won't be defined by the recession, but by our response to it. His voice is smooth and warm and practiced and every word is precisely calibrated to sound inspiring without committing to anything.

The parents love it. Standing ovation.

I watch Derek return to his seat, watch him accept the handshakes and shoulder-pats, and I think about what he said to me in February, sitting outside the library with cigarette smoke curling around his jaw: *Money is the only thing that's real.* I think about his dad losing the house. His mom's two jobs. The way Derek looked at the world and decided the thing to do was win.

He's not wrong. He's just solving the wrong problem.

Marcus, seven rows back, catches my eye and makes a face — crossed eyes, tongue out — and I almost laugh out loud in the middle of Jessica Demarco's name being called. He has no gold cords, no special honors. His GPA is fine. His engineering dreams are big and real and he'll pursue them at Iowa State in the fall, two hours south, where he'll be the only Black kid in half his classes and he'll build bridges anyway. Because that's what Marcus does.

They call my name. I walk across the stage. Principal Henderson shakes my hand and says something I don't hear. Camera flashes. Diane is definitely crying. I can't see her but I know. I know.

After — the parking lot, the chaos of families finding each other, the caps thrown upward in that clichéd arc that nonetheless feels like something when you're in it — after all that, Frank finds me. He's wearing his good flannel, the one with no paint on it, and his work boots are cleaned. He extends his hand.

A handshake. Not a hug. A handshake. Man to man. His calloused palm against mine, firm and dry, and he holds it for exactly the right amount of time, and he says, "Proud of you, bud," and that's it. That's the most physically demonstrative my father has ever been.

Diane wraps me in both arms and sobs into my gown and says about fifteen things I can't make out. Sophie stands to the side eating a piece of sheet cake from the refreshment table. She watches. She doesn't congratulate me. But she doesn't say anything sharp either, and from Sophie, silence is a gift.

And then Marcus, arm around my shoulders, cap in his free hand, grinning so wide I can count every tooth and the gap between them: "Everything starts now, right?"

I smile. It's real and it's fake and it's both at once, like everything I do.

"Yeah," I say. "Everything starts now."

I mean something completely different.

---

## Chapter 6: The Bookshelf and the Debt

The deck is rotting from the inside. You can't see it unless you know where to look — the surface boards are weathered but intact, the gray wood grain still pretty in a rustic way. But Frank peels back one of the planks and shows me the joists underneath, soft and dark with water damage, the grain separated like wet cardboard.

"See that?" He pokes a screwdriver into the wood and it sinks an inch without resistance. "Been going for years. Whole thing needs to come out."

It's the second week of June. Summer stretches ahead, featureless and hot. I'm eighteen years old and I have nowhere to be until August, when UNI orientation starts, and Frank has a plan for my time that involves manual labor and very few words.

"I'll get the sawzall," I say.

He nods. That's how it starts.

---

Demolition takes two days. We pry up deck boards and cut through nails with the reciprocating saw and stack the ruined lumber by the alley for bulk pickup. The joists come apart in chunks, punky and wet, and underneath is bare earth and gravel and the cinder block footings that Frank poured himself in 1997, which are fine. The foundation is fine. It's everything built on top that failed.

I don't think about the metaphor. I refuse to think about the metaphor.

Frank works without explaining. He assumes I can see what needs doing, and mostly I can — I'm thirty-five in a teenage body, I've held tools before, I understand leverage and sequence. But Frank doesn't know that. Frank thinks his son, who six months ago could barely make eye contact, is now hauling lumber and running a skill saw with quiet competence, and he doesn't ask why. He just adjusts. Hands me the next board. Holds the other end while I mark the cut line.

By the end of the second day my shoulders are sunburned and my hands are torn up. I haven't done this kind of work in this body. These muscles are new to the task. At dinner I can barely lift my fork and Sophie says, "You look like you got hit by a truck," and Frank says, "He's fine," and that's approval.

---

The new deck goes up over three weeks. Pressure-treated lumber, 5/4 decking, stainless steel screws instead of nails. Frank ordered everything from the lumber yard on 18th Street — he knows the guy, got a contractor discount even though he's not a contractor, just a pipe fitter who can build anything if you give him enough weekends.

We develop a rhythm. Mornings we start at seven, before the heat gets bad. Frank drinks his coffee on the porch — the front porch, the one that's not torn apart — and I eat whatever breakfast I can assemble, usually toast, sometimes cereal. By seven-fifteen we're in the backyard with the radio on, tuned to the classic rock station, and we work until noon when the sun gets directly overhead and the air goes thick and shimmering.

Afternoons I'm at the library. But mornings belong to the deck.

Frank doesn't talk much during the work. Instructions, mostly. "Hold that." "Other end." "Six inches from the edge." But sometimes, in the pauses — while we're drinking water or measuring a board for the third time because Frank measures everything three times — he talks.

"Pop was a pipe fitter too," he says one morning. We're sitting on the frame, legs dangling, the bare joists below us. "Started me when I was sixteen. Summers, then after school."

I know this. I knew this in the original timeline too, but it was background noise, family history I never asked about. Now I hold still and wait.

"He wasn't bad." Frank turns his water bottle in his hands, looking at the yard. The Johnsons' elm tree throws a long shadow. "He just didn't know how to be good. There's a difference."

I wait.

"Came home and he was tired. And when he was tired he drank. And when he drank he was..." Frank shrugs one shoulder. "Loud. He was loud."

This is more than Frank has ever told me. In either timeline. I understand that I'm receiving something precious and fragile, offered because the work has created a space where men can talk without facing each other, where the words can fall into the middle distance and not land anywhere too hard.

"I decided a long time ago," Frank says. "Whatever he was. I'd be something else."

He stands up, caps his water bottle, and picks up the drill. We go back to work. He doesn't say anything else about it, and I don't ask. But for the rest of the morning I'm thinking about the physics of inheritance — how a man can be shaped entirely by what he refused to repeat. How silence can be a choice, not an absence. How Frank's quiet is not the same as his father's loud. It's the precise opposite. It's the space left behind when you remove something dangerous and decide not to fill it with anything else.

---

I find the bookshelf on a Thursday.

I'm in the garage looking for the box of deck screws — Frank keeps his hardware organized by size in coffee cans on a shelf, but we've run through the 3-inch screws faster than expected. The garage is dim and hot and smells like gasoline and old sawdust. I'm reaching behind a stack of boxes near the back wall when my hand hits something smooth and flat.

I pull the boxes forward. Behind them, leaning against the wall at an angle, is a bookshelf. Pine. About four feet tall, three shelves, simple construction. The wood is hand-sanded — I can see the slight inconsistency of the grain, the places where the orbital sander missed and the hand had to go back. The joints are mortise and tenon. No nails. The whole thing was built with patience and a modest set of tools and it's slightly uneven — the left side is maybe a quarter inch taller than the right. You'd only notice if you put a level on it.

Frank built this. For me. When I was twelve.

I stand there in the garage with my hand on the top of it and I remember — the original version. Frank carried it up to my room one afternoon and I said something like "cool" or "thanks" and never put a single book on it. It ended up back in the garage within a year. Shoved behind boxes. Buried under the accumulation of family life. Frank never mentioned it again.

It's been back here for six years, collecting dust. The hand-sanded pine gone gray in places. A water stain on one side from a leak in the garage roof that Frank fixed in 2007.

I carry it out into the sunlight. It weighs nothing. It weighs everything.

---

I put it in my room. Between the window and the desk, where the light hits it in the afternoon. I wipe it down with a damp rag and put books on it — the disaster communication theory book from the library, my AP textbooks that I'm keeping for no good reason, the copy of *Ender's Game* that Marcus lent me sophomore year and I never returned, a beat-up paperback of Vonnegut's *Cat's Cradle* that I found at Goodwill last month. The shelf bows very slightly under the weight. The quarter-inch lean to the left is visible if you look for it.

Frank walks past my door that evening on the way to the bathroom. I hear his footsteps slow. Stop. I'm at my desk with a library book open, pretending to read. He stands in the doorway for maybe three seconds.

"Looks good in there," he says.

Then his footsteps continue down the hall. The bathroom door closes.

Three words. Five syllables. A man who built something for his son six years ago, saw it rejected without ceremony, said nothing about it, stored it without bitterness, and now — now sees it in use, holding weight, doing what it was made to do. And the only thing he says is *looks good in there.*

I wait until I hear the bathroom faucet running. Then I put my face into my pillow and I cry like I'm drowning. Not sad crying. Not happy crying either. Some third thing that doesn't have a name. The crying you do when someone has been loving you for years and you finally have the eyes to see it.

In the original timeline, that shelf rotted behind boxes until the garage itself was gone. Along with the house. Along with Frank. Along with everything.

I keep my face in the pillow until I can breathe normally. Then I wash my face in my bedroom sink and go downstairs and ask Frank if he wants to watch the Cardinals game. He says yes. We sit on the couch and watch baseball and he falls asleep in the fourth inning and I cover him with the afghan from the back of the couch and turn the volume down.

---

The library becomes my second job. Three afternoons a week, sometimes four. I have a table I think of as mine — back corner of the second floor, near the periodicals nobody reads, with a view of the parking lot and the early-summer trees going full and green.

I'm reading everything. SETI publications. Astrophysics journals. Signal detection theory. The Drake equation and its critics. Fermi's paradox and the zoo hypothesis and the dark forest theory. Papers with titles like "On the Statistical Properties of Narrowband Signals in Radio SETI" and "Bayesian Inference Applied to Anomalous Spectral Features."

I'm building a script.

Not a literal script — I'm not writing dialogue on index cards — but a framework. A way to walk into Dr. Judith Chen's office in a few months and say something that will make her listen. Something that isn't "I'm from the future and aliens are coming." Something that starts with a question and ends with her wanting to look at the coordinates I know by heart.

Her papers are good. Really good. She writes with a clarity unusual in astrophysics — no jargon for jargon's sake, every sentence doing work. Her most cited paper is on statistical methods for differentiating signal from noise in radio telescope data, published 2006, and it's exactly what someone would need if they were trying to identify a non-natural transmission in a very specific part of the sky.

I take notes in a spiral-bound notebook that I keep in my backpack at all times. The notes look like an overeager freshman trying to impress a professor. That's fine. That's the costume. Underneath the costume is a thirty-five-year-old man who watched the sky turn gray and who knows exactly which frequency, which coordinates, which date in 2011, the signal will arrive.

Two years from now. Just over two years. I have to be ready. I have to have someone credible standing next to me when I point at the sky and say *there.*

---

Evening. Late June. The deck is almost finished — just the railing left, which we'll do next week. The backyard smells like cut grass and the citronella candle Diane lit to keep the mosquitoes back. She's on the old porch swing we moved to the side yard while the deck was torn up. I'm sitting on the steps with a glass of lemonade.

"Come sit," she says.

I sit. The swing creaks. She pushes with one foot, a gentle motion that makes the chains sigh.

"I don't know what happened to you," she says.

I go very still.

"Sometime last fall. Maybe over summer. I don't know exactly when." She's not looking at me. She's looking at the yard, at the fireflies just starting to blink near the fence line. "But you're different."

"Mom—"

"Better," she says. "You're different and you're better. Kinder. Present." She pauses. "You used to be here but not here. Like you were waiting for something to start. And now you're... here."

I think about the absolute insane irony of that statement. I was here but not here. Correct. I was here, in this time, in this body, with my family who was alive and my future that hadn't happened yet, and I was sleepwalking through it because I was seventeen and seventeen-year-olds don't know they're living in the good part.

"I'm proud of you," Diane says. Simply. Like it's nothing.

I almost tell her. Right then, on the porch swing, with the fireflies and the citronella and the new deck behind us smelling like clean lumber — I almost open my mouth and say *Mom, I came back. I was thirty-five and the world ended and something sent me back here and I don't know why but I'm trying to fix it.* I can feel the words in my chest like a physical pressure. The relief of being known. Of being seen for what I actually am instead of this performance I'm locked inside.

I close my mouth. I look at the fireflies.

"Thanks, Mom," I say.

She reaches over and squeezes my hand once. Then she goes inside to finish the dishes. I sit on the swing and listen to the chains creak and watch the light drain out of the sky, and I think about all the people I can't tell and all the reasons I can't tell them and how the loneliness of this is not the worst thing. The worst thing would be telling them and having them look at me differently. The worst thing would be becoming something other than their son. Their brother. Their friend.

So I keep the secret. I keep all of them. They're stacking up like debts.

---

Night. My room. The books on Frank's shelf throw long shadows in the streetlight coming through the window. I can see the lean — the quarter inch, the imperfection, the evidence of a man doing his best with a hand saw and a square and love he couldn't say out loud.

I lie in bed and think: In the original version, I spent this summer getting drunk with Derek Holt. In that version, I never rebuilt the deck with Frank. Never heard him talk about his father. In that version, the shelf rotted in the garage and I rotted right along with it — slowly, invisibly, from the inside out, like the deck joists. The surface looked fine. The grain was pretty. But the weight-bearing parts were soft and dark and falling apart.

I'm not that anymore. I don't know what I am — time traveler, con artist, Cassandra in reverse — but I'm not that.

I turn off the light.

Tomorrow I start building something else. The deck was practice. The foundation is set. Now comes the frame — the network, the credibility, the relationships that will hold weight when the weight comes. Dr. Chen in the fall. Marcus after that. Maybe others. Slowly. Carefully. One board at a time, measured twice, screwed down with stainless steel so the weather can't pull it apart.

I have two years. It's not enough and it's everything.

I close my eyes. The bookshelf holds.
## Chapter 7: The Scientist and the Prophet

The thing about being twenty years old inside an eighteen-year-old body is that nobody warns you how boring it's going to be.

I'm sitting in Introduction to Emergency Management, room 214 of Sabin Hall, and Professor Hendricks is explaining the Incident Command System like it's a revelation. Unified command. Span of control. The five management functions. I already know this. Not because I'm smart — because where I come from, I watched these systems fail in real time, watched people die in the gap between theory and execution, and I spent years afterward thinking about why.

But I raise my hand. I ask questions that sound like a curious freshman's questions. I take notes in a spiral notebook with a ballpoint pen that costs forty-nine cents. I am the most focused student in every room I enter, and this makes me strange. The other freshmen are discovering beer pong and hookup culture and the exhilarating freedom of nobody checking whether you go to class. I go to every class. I sit in the second row. I do the reading before it's assigned.

My roommate, a kid from Dubuque named Tyler, asked me once if I was on something. "You just — you're always doing something. You never just, like, chill."

"I chill," I told him.

"Dude. You were reorganizing your desk at six a.m."

Fair point. But Tyler doesn't know that I can feel the clock. Every day that passes is a day closer to something he can't see, and the weight of that knowledge turns leisure into a luxury I can't afford. So I study. I volunteer. I run three miles every morning along the Cedar River trail while the campus is still sleeping. I eat in the dining hall alone, usually, a book propped against my water glass.

I am building something. I just can't tell anyone what.

---

The lecture is on a Thursday evening in October. "Anomalous Signal Detection in the Microwave Background: Challenges and Opportunities." It's in a small room on the third floor of McCollum Science Hall, the kind with a pull-down projector screen and seats for maybe forty. Twelve people show up, and three of them are clearly here because they thought it was the astronomy club meeting.

Dr. Judith Chen is not what I expected.

I mean — I'd seen her faculty photo. Read her papers. All six of them that I could access through the library database, plus two more I'd gotten through interlibrary loan from Iowa State. But the photo is a standard academic headshot, professionally lit, nothing notable. The woman at the front of the room is wearing a Ramones t-shirt under a corduroy blazer that looks like she stole it from a 1970s estate sale. Her hair is pulled back in a clip that's losing the fight. She's maybe forty-five, thin, quick-moving. When she talks, her hands describe shapes in the air like she's conducting an orchestra only she can hear.

And she's brilliant. Obviously, incandescently brilliant. Within two minutes of opening her mouth, she's left three of the astronomy-club kids behind and the nine remaining audience members are split between genuine comprehension and polite confusion.

I am comprehending.

Not because I understand the math — I don't, not really, not the way she does. But because I know what she's reaching toward. I know where her research leads. In the timeline I came from, Dr. Judith Chen publishes a paper in 2012 that gets modest attention and significant skepticism, and by 2015 she's vindicated in ways that nobody living through it will fully appreciate until much later. She's one of the first. She doesn't know it yet.

She's talking about spectral anomalies in data from the Allen Telescope Array. Patterns that don't match known sources. The signal-to-noise problem. How do you distinguish genuine anomalies from instrument artifacts, atmospheric interference, the cosmic microwave background's own quirks?

"The challenge," she says, pacing, "isn't detection. We detect thousands of anomalies every year. The challenge is classification. The challenge is having the intellectual honesty to say: I don't know what this is, and the courage to keep looking at it rather than filing it under 'noise' and moving on."

After the lecture, most of the twelve drift out. I wait. I'm not the last to leave — that would be conspicuous. I'm third-to-last, lingering near the front as two actual physics grad students ask her about her ATA access schedule.

When they leave, I step forward.

"Dr. Chen? I had a question about your work on the 1420-megahertz exclusion zone."

She's gathering her notes, laptop half-closed. She looks up. "The hydrogen line exclusion? That's not really what the talk was about."

"No. But it's related to the classification problem. You mentioned instrument artifacts — but in your 2007 paper, you flagged three anomalies in the water-hole band that you couldn't attribute to any known source or any known artifact. Two of them were in a spectral range that overlaps with the exclusion zone margins."

She stops gathering. Looks at me properly for the first time. I'm an eighteen-year-old in a hoodie and jeans, backpack slung over one shoulder. I look like every other freshman on this campus.

"That paper wasn't published," she says carefully.

It was, actually. In a conference proceedings volume with a distribution of maybe two hundred. I found it through a citation chain that took me three weeks to trace. But that's not the answer that gets me what I need.

"I read the conference proceedings from the 2007 IAU regional meeting," I say. "It's in the UNI library's digital archive. But what interested me was the pattern. The two signals you flagged — they were both in the 1400 to 1427 range, both brief, both at similar right-ascension coordinates. You called them 'unclassified transients' in the paper. But you didn't publish a follow-up."

She's very still now. The laptop is forgotten. "What's your name?"

"Nate Callahan. I'm a freshman. Emergency management."

"Emergency management." She says it like she's tasting something unexpected.

"Yeah. But I — I've been reading. About signal detection. About the classification problem. And I've been thinking about those two transients you flagged."

"What about them."

Here's the moment. The tightrope walk. Too much and she'll think I'm a crank. Too little and I'm just another undergrad who read one paper and thinks he's discovered something. I need to be exactly strange enough to be interesting.

"I think the reason you didn't publish a follow-up is that when you went back to look for them, they weren't there anymore. And that bothered you. Because instrument artifacts are repeatable, and astrophysical sources don't just vanish. But a transient signal from a moving source would only appear in a specific window, and if you didn't know the trajectory, you wouldn't know when to look again."

The room is silent. The fluorescent lights hum. Dr. Chen is looking at me like I've just spoken in a language she didn't know anyone else spoke.

"Where did you read about that?" she asks. Her voice is flat. Controlled.

"I didn't read about it," I say. "I thought about it."

A long pause. Then she does something I don't expect — she laughs. Short, sharp, almost surprised out of herself.

"Office hours are Tuesday and Thursday, two to four," she says. "Room 318. Bring that brain."

---

I go on a Thursday. Then the following Tuesday. Then it becomes a pattern.

Her office is a disaster. Papers everywhere, books stacked horizontally on shelves because the vertical space is full. A whiteboard covered in equations I can follow for about two lines before they lose me. A poster of the Arecibo message on the wall behind her desk. And Boltzmann — a massive orange tabby who occupies the only visitor's chair like he owns it, which, to be fair, he does.

The first few visits, Chen tests me. She doesn't make it obvious, but I can feel it. She recommends papers. Not introductory ones — research-level work, the kind graduate students struggle with. I read them. I don't pretend to understand all the math, because pretending would be stupid and she'd see through it instantly. But I understand the logic. The frameworks. The implications.

"You're not a physics student," she says one afternoon in November, watching me scratch Boltzmann's chin while we discuss Bayesian approaches to signal classification.

"No."

"Why not?"

"Because I'm not a physicist. I don't have the math. And I don't have time to get the math — not and do what I actually need to do."

"Which is what? Emergency management?" She says it without condescension, but with genuine curiosity.

"Yeah. I want to be useful when things go wrong. That's — that's what I'm building toward."

"And this?" She gestures at the papers spread between us. "What's this, then?"

"This is something else. This is me needing to understand something that I think is important, and you being the person who can help me understand it."

She leans back. Studies me. I'm used to people looking at me like I'm a puzzle, but Chen does it differently. There's no suspicion in it. Just a sharp, academic curiosity.

"You know things you shouldn't know," she says. "Not just that conference proceedings — that was findable. But the trajectory analysis. The spectral overlay pattern. You think like someone who already has a hypothesis and is working backward to establish evidence."

"Is that a problem?"

"It's a methodology problem. It's how cranks work. Start with the conclusion, cherry-pick the data."

"And what if the hypothesis is correct?"

She smiles. "Then it's still a methodology problem. But it's a more interesting one."

---

December. January. February. The semester turns. I take Chen's Introduction to Astrophysics as an elective, officially becoming her student as well as whatever else I am to her — project? Curiosity? I sit in the front row and I'm the only non-physics-major in the room. I earn a B+, which is honest. The math limits me.

But the office hours continue. And in those sessions, we're not doing coursework. We're talking about detection methodologies, about the difference between looking and finding, about what you do when the data suggests something your priors say is impossible.

She tells me about Boltzmann one afternoon — named for the constant, of course, but also because "he operates according to his own statistical mechanics. His behavior is technically deterministic but practically unpredictable."

The cat is asleep on a stack of Nature back issues. He doesn't look unpredictable. He looks like a fur-covered throw pillow.

I am being careful. Every week, I walk the line. I show enough knowledge to keep her engaged but not so much that she gets frightened. I ask questions that lead her toward conclusions I already know are correct but let her arrive there under her own power. I am, in the most precise and damning sense of the word, manipulating her.

I hate it. But I don't stop.

---

March comes in cold and wet. Cedar Falls is still buried under dirty snow. I sit in Chen's office with my hands wrapped around a mug of terrible departmental coffee and I know it's time.

I've been building to this for five months. Every paper I've read, every conversation we've had, every carefully constructed question — it's all been scaffolding for this moment. The moment I stop being an unusually perceptive undergraduate and become something she doesn't have a category for.

"I want to tell you something," I say.

Chen is grading, red pen in hand. She doesn't look up immediately. "Go ahead."

"There's going to be a signal. An anomalous signal, in approximately eighteen months. Maybe a little less. It'll be in the water-hole band, similar spectral profile to the two transients from your 2007 paper."

Now she looks up.

"I know the approximate coordinates," I continue. "Right ascension and declination. I don't know the exact timing — I'm working from something closer to an educated guess than a prediction. But the coordinates I'm confident about."

She puts the red pen down slowly. Boltzmann, sensing some shift in the room's temperature, lifts his head.

"Nate."

"I know how this sounds."

"Do you? Because it sounds like you're either telling me you have access to a detection pipeline I don't know about, or you're telling me something much stranger than that."

"I don't have access to any pipeline. I'm an emergency management major who reads your papers and borrows your cat."

"Then how—"

"I can't explain it. Not yet. Not in a way that would satisfy you as a scientist, and I respect you too much to give you a bad explanation and ask you to accept it on faith."

She's quiet for a long moment. The office is still except for Boltzmann's raspy breathing and the tick of the wall clock.

"Give me the coordinates."

I recite them. She writes them on a Post-it note. Her handwriting is cramped, precise — not what you'd expect from someone whose office looks like a paper avalanche.

"If this signal appears where you say it will," she says, sticking the Post-it to her monitor, "I'm going to need a real explanation."

"If it appears," I say, "you'll get one."

She holds my gaze for another moment. I can see the war behind her eyes — the scientist who knows this is absurd, fighting the scientist who recognizes that I've been right about too many things to dismiss. Finally she nods once, a short sharp motion.

"Okay. Eighteen months."

"Give or take."

"I'll be watching."

I stand, swing my backpack over my shoulder. At the door I stop.

"Dr. Chen?"

"Yes?"

"Thank you. For — all of this. For not throwing me out."

She picks up the red pen again, returns to grading as if nothing has happened. "I'm a scientist, Nate. I don't throw out data."

---

That night I'm back in my dorm room, Tyler gone somewhere — party, girlfriend's room, doesn't matter. I'm alone. The ceiling is popcorn textured and institutional. My desk lamp throws a circle of light onto the notebook I've been writing in since August.

I haven't written about the future in this notebook. Nothing that could be found and misread. It's not a record. It's more like — therapy. Working through the knot.

Tonight I write:

*The man who can save the world and the man who deserves to be saved cannot be the same person.*

Because what am I doing? Let's be honest. Let's be actually, brutally honest in a way I can't be with anyone, not Marcus, not Chen, not even myself most days.

I'm manipulating people. That's what this is. I identified Chen as an asset. I engineered a meeting. I cultivated a relationship over months, feeding her information at exactly the rate that would maximize her investment in me. I did it consciously, deliberately, with the specific goal of positioning myself so that when the signal arrives, I have a credible channel to the scientific community.

This is manipulation. It's textbook. The fact that I genuinely like her — genuinely respect her work, genuinely enjoy Boltzmann's presence, genuinely look forward to our conversations — doesn't change what I'm doing. It might actually make it worse. Because it means I'm not even cold about it. I'm warm and human and enjoying the friendship I'm building on a foundation of calculated deception.

I could tell her the truth. Time travel. The future. All of it. But the truth, unearned, sounds like insanity. And insanity gets you institutionalized, not listened to. So I dole out proof in fragments. I let her arrive at trust through accumulated evidence. I play the long game because the long game is the only game that works.

Manipulation is my tool. Honesty is my goal. The toolkit and the mission are at war with each other, and the battlefield is every relationship I have.

*I have to be both*, I write. *I have to be the manipulator who earns trust through strategic disclosure, AND the honest man who deserves that trust. I have to hold both things at once.*

*Anyway. The point is—*

I stop writing. The pen hovers. I don't know how to finish the sentence.

Maybe that's the point. Maybe the point is that there isn't one. Maybe the point is just: keep going. Keep building. Keep being careful and calculating and guilty about it, and hope that eventually the calculation serves something real enough to justify the cost.

I close the notebook. I turn off the lamp. In the dark, the digital clock reads 1:47 a.m. and I have an 8 a.m. class.

I don't sleep for a long time.

---

## Chapter 8: The Long Game

Fall 2010. Sophomore year. I am nineteen years old for the second time in my life and it is exactly as unremarkable as it sounds.

The emergency management program at UNI is small — maybe forty students across all years — and I've made myself into the department's quiet overachiever. I have my FEMA Professional Development Series certificates. I've done Red Cross disaster response training. I volunteer at the local chapter twice a week, which mostly means organizing supply closets and updating spreadsheets, but occasionally means driving to a house fire at 2 a.m. and handing blankets to a family who just lost everything.

Those nights are the ones that feel real. Not the coursework — that's infrastructure, necessary but bloodless. The actual disasters, even small ones, even house fires and car accidents and the grandmother who fell and lay on her kitchen floor for sixteen hours before anyone checked. Those remind me why I'm building this. There's a version of preparation that's purely abstract, and then there's the version where you hand a woman a bottle of water and a blanket and she grips your arm with fingers like bird bones and says *thank you, honey, thank you*.

I write a paper on community resilience in rural flood zones. Professor Hendricks calls it "publishable with revision." I revise it. It doesn't get published — I'm an undergraduate, the journals don't take undergraduates seriously — but the research goes into my files. Into the architecture.

I take three FEMA Independent Study courses online, the self-paced ones: IS-230 (Fundamentals of Emergency Management), IS-235 (Emergency Planning), IS-240 (Leadership and Influence). I study them like scripture. Not because the material is new to me — most of it isn't — but because having the certificates matters. Credentials are armor. When the time comes, when I need people to listen, the letters after my name will buy me thirty seconds of attention I wouldn't get otherwise.

This is what preparation looks like up close. Not montage-worthy. Not cinematic. It's a guy in a dorm room at eleven p.m., clicking through online modules about resource typing and mutual aid agreements, while his roommate watches basketball on a laptop across the room.

Tyler moved out after freshman year. My new roommate, Dave, is quieter, less curious. We coexist. It works.

---

June 2010. The Cedar River rises.

Not catastrophically — this isn't 2008, when the river crested thirteen feet above flood stage and drowned Cedar Rapids. But it's bad enough. Record rainfall in May pushes the river up, and by the first week of June the National Weather Service is issuing flood warnings for several tributaries.

I'm in the Red Cross staging area at the Waterloo convention center at 5 a.m. on a Thursday when the call comes to deploy volunteers for sandbagging along the river south of Cedar Falls. I grab my gear — work gloves, boots, rain jacket — and I'm in the van with six other volunteers before anyone has to ask twice.

The sandbagging site is chaos when we arrive. Not dangerous chaos — organizational chaos. Too many volunteers, not enough coordination. People standing around waiting for instructions. Stacks of empty sandbags in one location, fill dirt in another, and the actual levee line two hundred yards from either.

Something in me clicks. The same thing that clicked when I watched the systems fail before, when I studied the after-action reports and thought *this is where it broke down, this is the seam*.

I start moving. Not ordering — I don't have authority and I know better than to pretend I do. But I start a chain. I position myself between the fill dirt and the bag station and I say, loud enough: "Hey — if you're waiting, grab a bag from that stack, fill it here, pass it down. We can move twice as fast in a line."

And people do it. Not because I'm in charge but because human beings in uncertain situations want someone — anyone — to say the next obvious thing. The chain forms. Bags move. Other chains start forming nearby, copying the pattern.

By noon we've placed six hundred bags along a quarter-mile stretch of riverbank. My shoulders ache. My back is going to hate me tomorrow. My boots are caked in mud that goes halfway up my shins. And I feel more like myself than I have in months.

Not because I used future knowledge. I didn't. There's nothing from the future in "form a line and pass the bags." That's just logistics. That's just seeing a problem and saying the obvious thing and being willing to be the first one to start doing it.

Maybe that's who I actually am, underneath all the manipulation and calculated disclosure and strategic relationship-building. Maybe that's the version of me that deserves to survive this.

Or maybe I'm just good at organizing people, and I'm romanticizing it because I'm desperate for one thing in my life that isn't tainted by foreknowledge.

---

Sunday dinner at home. July. The deck Frank and I built last summer has weathered well — the stain has darkened slightly, but the boards are solid, the railing sturdy. We eat on it when the weather's good. Tonight it's good. Mom made chicken and corn on the cob and there's a pitcher of lemonade sweating in the center of the table.

Sophie is sixteen and has finally gotten her braces off. Her smile is different without them — more confident, or maybe just less self-conscious. She's on her phone half the time, which Mom allows at outdoor dinners but not indoor ones, a distinction I don't fully understand but don't question.

Frank asks about school while he eats, methodical bites between questions. "So what's this semester look like?"

"More specialized stuff. Hazard mitigation planning. Exercise design — that's where you simulate disasters and test response systems."

"Simulate disasters," Frank repeats. "Like practice runs."

"Exactly like practice runs. So when real ones happen, people know what to do."

He nods, chewing corn. Thinks about it. "So you're learning how to help people when things go bad?"

"Yeah, Dad."

He sets down the cob. Wipes his mouth with a napkin. "That's... well. That's good."

It's not effusive. Frank doesn't do effusive. But the pause before "well" carries weight. It's the pause of a man who spent months not understanding what his son was doing, and now understanding it in simple enough terms to approve. *You help people when things go bad.* That's something a pipe fitter's son does. That makes sense.

Sophie glances up from her phone. She's spotted the FEMA manuals on the counter inside — I brought them home to study over the weekend. "You're so weird now," she says. "You have, like, government textbooks."

"They're training manuals."

"Same difference. Nobody our age reads government training manuals for fun."

"It's not for fun."

"That's even weirder."

But she's not hostile. That's the thing. A year ago, Sophie looked at me like I was a stranger wearing her brother's face. Now she looks at me like I'm her brother who happens to be inexplicably, entertainingly strange. That's progress. I'll take bewilderment over suspicion any day.

Mom catches my eye across the table and smiles. She doesn't say anything. She doesn't need to.

---

September. Marcus calls on a Sunday night, which is our pattern now. Once a week, sometimes every ten days, whenever the engineering workload lets him surface.

"Dude, you won't believe this lab I'm in," he says. "We had to build a truss bridge out of balsa wood and then load-test it until failure. Mine held forty-seven pounds before it snapped."

"Is that good?"

"It's third in the class. Which is pissing me off because Mike Chen's held fifty-two and I know his glue joints were sloppy. There's no justice."

I'm at my desk, textbook open but forgotten. I'm smiling. Marcus's voice is animated the way it gets when he's excited about something, the words tumbling over each other. He's found his thing. Engineering. The certainty of math applied to physical problems. The satisfaction of building something that either holds or doesn't.

"You'll beat him next time," I say.

"Damn right. I'm redesigning the entire truss geometry. You should see my sketches — I've been up since six working on the force diagrams."

"Force diagrams at six a.m. on a Sunday. And you call me intense."

He laughs. Gap-toothed, warm. "Fair. Hey — how's your semester going? Still doing the disaster stuff?"

"Still doing the disaster stuff."

"When the apocalypse comes, I'm calling you first."

He says it like a joke. I let it be a joke.

We talk for another twenty minutes. He tells me about a girl in his dynamics class. I tell him about the sandbagging, which he finds genuinely impressive. "You're out there actually doing it. That's cool, man. That's real."

After we hang up, I sit for a while in the quiet of my room. Marcus is my friend. Genuinely. Not strategically — I don't need anything from him. He's just my friend, and I'm his, and we maintain this thing across the distance through weekly phone calls and honest conversation. It's maybe the only relationship in my life that isn't contaminated by what I know.

I want to keep it that way.

---

Winter again. Spring. The months compress into routine: classes, volunteering, office hours with Chen (which continue, though we don't discuss the prediction directly — it sits between us like a sealed envelope, waiting). I study. I run. I attend to the architecture of my preparation with the grim patience of someone laying bricks for a building whose blueprints only he can see.

It's not glamorous. I want to be clear about that. There's no montage here. No swelling soundtrack. It's me, alone in a library carrel at 9 p.m. on a Friday, reading a journal article about post-disaster housing displacement while everyone else my age is out living their lives. It's me driving to the Red Cross chapter on a Tuesday afternoon when I could be napping. It's me declining invitations — not many, I'm not popular, but occasionally someone asks — because I need the hours.

I chose sobriety eighteen months ago and it's stuck. That's something. Most days it's easy. Some days it's not. The days it's not are usually the days when the loneliness gets acute, when the gulf between who I am and who everyone thinks I am feels uncrossable, when I want to be twenty-year-old Nate at a bar with friends instead of twenty-year-old-in-an-eighteen-year-old's-body Nate in a dorm room with FEMA manuals.

But I don't drink. I run instead. Or I call Marcus. Or I go to Chen's office and argue about Bayesian priors while Boltzmann sheds on my jeans.

The long game is long. That's the whole point of it. You don't get to skip ahead.

---

Spring 2011. The envelope between Chen and me begins to weigh something.

We don't discuss it directly, but I notice her mentioning the Allen Telescope Array more often. She's secured observation time — I don't ask how, don't want to know what favors she called in. She mentions it offhandedly: "I've been running some monitoring scripts on my ATA allocation." She doesn't say *for the coordinates you gave me*. She doesn't have to.

I start sleeping badly in April. By May it's worse. The signal — if it exists, if my memory is right, if the timeline hasn't diverged in some way I can't account for — should appear sometime in the late spring or summer of 2011. I've given Chen a window. Approximately eighteen months from March 2010. That's September 2011 at the outside. But my memory isn't precise. It could be earlier.

Every morning I wake up and check my phone. No missed calls. No emails from Chen. The absence of contact is both relief and torture.

I eat less. I study more. I run longer — four miles, five, six. Tyler-now-Dave notices nothing because Dave notices nothing, which is why Dave is an excellent roommate.

June comes. The corn grows tall in the fields outside Cedar Falls, that lush impossibility of the Iowa summer, everything green and heavy and alive. I have a summer research position with the emergency management department — data analysis, grant writing, unglamorous work. I sit in the department office with the air conditioning too cold and I stare at spreadsheets and I think about a point in space where, maybe, right now, something is pulsing in the water-hole band.

Or not. Maybe I'm wrong. Maybe I've built this entire architecture on a foundation of false memory, and the signal never comes, and Chen writes me off as a creative fraud, and I'm just a kid with emergency management certificates and a decade of phantom memories that don't correspond to anything real.

That thought should comfort me. If I'm wrong, nobody dies. If I'm wrong, the future I'm trying to prevent doesn't exist.

It doesn't comfort me. Because I'm not wrong. I know I'm not wrong the same way I know my own name: with a certainty that lives below reason, in the body, in the bones.

The signal is coming.

---

Tuesday, June 14th, 2011. 11:07 p.m.

I'm in bed, not sleeping. Reading, or trying to — a paperback I've read three pages of without absorbing a word. Dave is asleep across the room, his breathing slow and even. The window is open and warm air drifts in carrying the smell of cut grass and distant barbecue.

My phone rings.

The caller ID says DR. CHEN. I've never given her my cell number for social calls. She has it because it's on my student file, which she can access because I'm enrolled in her course. She has never used it before.

I answer before the second ring.

"Hello?"

"Nate." Her voice is — I don't know how to describe it. Controlled. Deliberately controlled, the way a person speaks when they're trying very hard not to sound the way they feel. "I need you to listen carefully."

"I'm listening."

"I found it."

The room goes very still. Even Dave's breathing seems to pause, though that's impossible.

"It's exactly where you said it would be," she continues. "Right ascension, declination, spectral profile — all within your parameters. I've verified it three times. It's not an artifact. It's not a known source. It's real."

I'm sitting up now. The paperback has fallen to the floor. My hand holding the phone is steady, which surprises me, because my chest is doing something complicated.

Silence on the line. Long enough that she might think the call dropped. Then:

"Who are you?" she asks.

Not *what is this.* Not *how did you know.* Who are you. Because she's a scientist and she's spent the last fourteen months watching the coordinates I gave her, and now the signal has arrived precisely as predicted, and the only unknown variable in her equation is me.

I close my eyes. The warm night presses in. Somewhere outside, a car door slams. Dave shifts in his sleep.

"Can we meet tomorrow?" I say. "In person. I'll tell you everything."

Another silence. Then: "My office. Eight a.m."

"I'll be there."

She hangs up without saying goodbye. I sit in the dark with the phone in my hand and my heart hammering and the absolute bone-deep certainty that everything — everything — changes now.

The long game is over. Whatever comes next is something else.
## Chapter 9: The Thing About Trust

The thing about having your entire worldview validated is that it doesn't feel the way you'd expect. I'd spent fifteen months waiting for Chen's call. Fifteen months of FEMA coursework and flood mitigation seminars and pretending to be a normal twenty-year-old who just happened to be really, really into emergency preparedness. Fifteen months of watching the calendar and thinking: *she'll find it. She has to find it.*

And then she did. June 14, 2011. "I found it. It's exactly where you said it would be."

I was in my dorm room — the one I shared with Dave, who was already asleep across the room — and felt nothing. Or not nothing. I felt the particular numbness of a man who has just been proven right about something he desperately wished he were wrong about.

Because here's what nobody tells you about Cassandra: she didn't *want* to be right about Troy.

"Nate? Are you there?"

"Yeah." My voice came out flat. "Yeah, I'm here. That's — congratulations, Dr. Chen."

A pause. Then, careful: "We need to talk. My office. Eight a.m."

"I'll be there."

She hung up. I sat on the edge of my bed — a mattress on a metal frame, no box spring, because I am twenty years old and live like it — and stared at the wall for a long time. The wall was beige. There was a water stain in the upper corner shaped like nothing, because water stains aren't shaped like things, that's just a thing people say to make the world feel more significant than it is.

The signal was real. The anomaly I'd described from memories of a future that hadn't happened yet — or had happened, or would happen, depending on how you think about time, which I try not to — was sitting in Chen's data, verified and undeniable. A blip in the water-hole band, exactly where I'd said, exactly when I'd said.

Which meant the rest of it was real too. All of it. Every terrible thing I remembered was coming.

I went to the bathroom and threw up.

---

Chen's office hadn't changed. Still the topographic maps, still the Ramones poster, still Boltzmann the orange tabby asleep on a stack of journals. But Chen herself had changed. When I walked in at 7:55 the next morning, she was sitting behind her desk with her hands folded, and the look on her face wasn't curiosity anymore. It was something harder. Something that had edges.

"Sit down," she said.

I sat. The same chair as before — green vinyl, cracked armrest. Boltzmann opened one eye, decided I was irrelevant, closed it again.

"I've run three independent analyses," Chen said. "Different methods, different baselines. The signal is real. It is not noise, it is not an artifact, it is not terrestrial interference." She picked up a pen, turned it between her fingers. "I submitted a preliminary report to SETI's post-detection protocol working group."

"And?"

"They think I'm chasing ghosts. The signal-to-noise ratio is borderline. Any single one of my analyses would be dismissed. Together they're suggestive but not conclusive." She set the pen down. "I know what I found, Nate. And I know that you told me where to find it."

Here it was. The moment I'd been constructing and reconstructing in my head for over a year. The explanation I needed to give that was true enough to maintain trust and vague enough to not get me committed.

"You asked me once who I am," I said.

"I remember."

"I'm going to give you a partial answer. And it's going to be unsatisfying. And I need you to accept it anyway."

Chen leaned back. Her blazer today was gray corduroy, worn at the elbows. Her expression said: *try me.*

"I have foreknowledge of events I cannot explain the source of," I said. The words felt rehearsed, which they were. Hundreds of times, in the shower, on walks, lying awake at three a.m. "I need you to trust the data, not the mechanism."

The silence that followed was the kind that has texture. Boltzmann's purring. The hum of Chen's ancient desktop computer. Somewhere in the building, a door closing.

"That," Chen said finally, "is the opposite of science."

"I know."

"You're asking me to accept an unfalsifiable premise."

"I'm asking you to look at the evidence. I told you where to point the telescope. The signal was there. I didn't plant it. I don't have the technology to fake an extraterrestrial radio signature. So either I'm the most elaborate con artist in the history of academic fraud, or I knew something I shouldn't have been able to know."

She studied me. I let her. There was nothing else I could do — no card left to play except sitting in this cracked vinyl chair and being looked at by a woman smart enough to know I wasn't lying and disciplined enough to hate that she couldn't explain why.

"Foreknowledge," she said, like she was testing the word for structural integrity. "Of events."

"Yes."

"Plural."

"Yes."

"The signal isn't the only thing."

"No."

Another silence. Chen reached down and scratched Boltzmann behind the ears. The cat stretched, resettled.

"I need to think about this," she said.

"Take your time."

"Come back Friday."

---

So began our weekly meetings. Every Friday, nine a.m., Chen's office. Boltzmann as witness. Sometimes she asked questions I could answer. Sometimes she asked questions I wouldn't answer. She kept meticulous notes in a leather-bound journal she never let me see.

I'll say this for Chen: she was angry, and she stayed anyway. That's not a small thing. Most people, when you hand them something that doesn't fit their framework, will either reject it outright or swallow it whole. Chen did neither. She held it at arm's length and examined it from every angle, prodding for weaknesses, and when she couldn't find any, she got pissed off about it and came back the next week to try again.

"I don't believe in psychic phenomena," she said one Friday in August.

"Neither do I."

"I don't believe in divine revelation."

"Same."

"Then what am I supposed to do with you?"

I shrugged. "Keep testing the data. That's all I'm asking. When I tell you something is going to happen, write it down. When it happens, compare. Build a sample size."

"That's not—" She stopped. Took off her glasses, cleaned them on her shirt. Put them back on. "That's actually reasonable."

"Stopped clock," I said.

She almost smiled. Almost.

---

The broader astronomy community, as predicted, dismissed Chen's signal. A polite form letter from the working group. A less polite email from a colleague at Berkeley who called her analysis "creative but premature." She didn't tell me about that one — I saw it on her screen one Friday when she'd forgotten to minimize her email.

I didn't bring it up. Some dignities you let people keep.

But she didn't stop working on it. That was the thing about Chen. She had the kind of stubbornness that looks like patience from the outside — the slow, grinding, geological certainty that she was right and the world would eventually catch up. I recognized it because I had a version of it too, though mine was less principled and more born of desperation.

By October, she'd written a paper she couldn't publish. By November, she'd developed a monitoring protocol for the signal's frequency band that she ran on university equipment during off-hours, technically unauthorized but not technically illegal. By December, she'd started asking me about timelines.

"If this foreknowledge of yours includes a schedule," she said, "I need to know how long I have."

"For what?"

"To get people to listen."

I looked at her. This slight woman in her cluttered office, with her orange cat and her unpublishable paper, asking me to hand her a countdown she couldn't possibly prepare for.

"You have time," I said. Which was true and also a lie, because time is the one thing none of us have enough of, and the amount Chen had was less than she deserved.

---

The fishing trip happened in September, when the air was still warm but the light had shifted to that particular amber that means summer is done pretending it's going to last.

Frank called on a Tuesday. "Hey, bud. Saturday look good? Thought maybe the Cedar."

"Yeah. Yeah, that sounds good."

"Early. Five-thirty."

"I'll be there."

So Saturday at five-fifteen I was standing in my parents' driveway in the dark, because Frank Callahan means five-thirty when he says five-thirty and if you're not ready when the truck starts, you're getting left behind. Some things haven't changed across timelines.

The truck smelled the same as it always did — coffee, Armor All, the ghost of pipe dope from Frank's work clothes. He handed me a thermos without a word. I poured myself a cup. We drove east toward the river.

The thing about Frank and me is that we don't need conversation. Not anymore. The deck project in 2009 fixed something between us that I'd spent a decade breaking in my first life, and now we existed in a space that was mostly silence and proximity. Which sounds lonely but isn't. It's the opposite of lonely. It's the thing you get when you've stopped performing for someone and they've stopped expecting you to.

The Cedar River at dawn in September is nothing like a postcard. It's muddy and flat and the banks are lined with scrubby cottonwoods that drop their leaves early, so there's always a film of decomposing vegetation on the surface near the shore. It smells like earth and tannins and, faintly, the livestock operations upstream. It's not beautiful. It's just a river.

We found a spot Frank liked — a shallow gravel bar where you could wade out in hip boots and cast into the deeper channel. He'd been fishing the Cedar for thirty years. He didn't catch much and didn't care. That wasn't the point.

We waded out. Cast. The line made that particular sound — the whisper of monofilament through air, the small *plop* at the end. Frank did it with the economy of long practice. I did it with the approximation of someone who'd been taught twice, once at twelve and once at eighteen, and still wasn't very good.

Forty minutes of silence. The sun came up slowly, the way it does in Iowa — no drama, no explosion of color, just a gradual lightening from gray to gold. A heron stood downstream, motionless and judgmental.

"Your grandfather," Frank said, apropos of nothing.

I waited.

"Your mom's dad, you know. Good man. Generous." He reeled in, cast again. "My old man wasn't like that."

This was new territory. Frank didn't talk about his father. In my first life, I'd gathered fragments — drinker, factory worker, dead at fifty-eight from a heart that gave out. But Frank had never narrated it. Never laid it down in sequence.

"He wasn't bad," Frank said. "That's what people never get. He wasn't a bad man. He just—" A long pause. The line drifted in the current. "He didn't know how to be good. Didn't have the equipment for it."

I stood very still. The water moved around my boots, cold through the rubber.

"Came home from work and sat in his chair and drank and that was it. That was the whole relationship. Thirty years of sitting in a chair." Frank adjusted his cap. "And I told myself I'd be different. I'd be present." A dry sound that might have been a laugh. "Turns out present doesn't mean the same thing as good at it."

"Dad—"

"No, it's—" He waved a hand. "I'm not looking for anything. I'm just saying. You turn out the way you turn out, and some of it's choice and some of it's just the shape you grew into." He looked at me. Frank's eyes are blue, same as mine. In the early light they looked almost gray. "You changed your shape, bud. Couple years ago. I don't know what happened and I'm not asking. But I see it."

My throat was tight. I looked at the river because I couldn't look at him.

"This is nice," Frank said. "Isn't it?"

"Yeah," I managed.

"Good." He nodded. Cast again. "That's good, bud."

We fished for another two hours. Caught three smallmouth between us, all under size, all released. Frank told me a story about a pipe job that went wrong in Des Moines — a pressurized line that blew and covered the foreman in twenty-year-old rust water. He laughed telling it. I laughed hearing it. The heron caught a fish, swallowed it whole in that grotesque unhinging way herons do, and flew downstream.

On the drive home Frank played the radio — classic country, Merle Haggard and George Jones — and I leaned my head against the window and thought about all the versions of this morning that never happened. The version where I was twenty and hungover and wouldn't have answered the phone. The version where Frank stopped trying to call. The version where we ran out of time without ever standing in a river together and saying the things that aren't *I love you* but mean the same thing.

He dropped me off at my apartment. "Next month?" he said.

"Yeah. Definitely."

He nodded. Drove away. I stood on the sidewalk holding my rod and tackle box, and I was crying, just a little, in the way where you don't wipe your face because acknowledging it makes it real.

Anyway. The point is—

---

October. November. December. The semesters blurred together in a way they hadn't before. I was taking a full course load — emergency management core requirements, plus electives in public administration and nonprofit law — and simultaneously running a study group, volunteering with the county emergency management office, and meeting Chen every Friday.

I slept five hours a night. Not because I was manic or driven by some heroic purpose. Because I was afraid. The clock was always ticking, and every hour I spent sleeping was an hour I wasn't building the thing that might save people.

That's not healthy. I knew it wasn't healthy. But healthy is a luxury for people who don't know what's coming.

In January 2012, I sat down with a legal pad and wrote the words: NORTHEAST REGIONAL AWARENESS NETWORK.

It was, deliberately, the most boring name I could think of. Not "Survive the Future." Not "Last Warning Coalition." Not anything that sounded like a prepper militia or a conspiracy theory or a guy who'd read too much science fiction. Just: a network. For awareness. In the northeast region. Of what? Of whatever. Natural disasters. Infrastructure vulnerabilities. Supply chain disruptions. The kind of thing that got funded by state emergency management grants and didn't set off anyone's weirdo alarm.

NEORAN. Even the acronym was boring. I loved it.

The 501(c)(3) paperwork took three months. I know that sounds simple — fill out forms, mail them in, wait — but nonprofit formation is an exercise in bureaucratic endurance. Articles of incorporation. Bylaws. Conflict of interest policies. A board of directors that had to include at least three unrelated individuals who weren't getting paid.

I listed myself as executive director. Marcus agreed to be on the board without really understanding what he was agreeing to ("Dude, yes, obviously, put my name on your apocalypse thing, this rules"). I found two other people — a retired county supervisor I'd worked with during flood response, and a local business owner who'd lost his shop in the 2008 floods and understood, viscerally, that preparedness wasn't paranoia.

And I listed Chen as scientific advisor.

"What does that entail?" she asked, when I showed her the paperwork.

"Right now? Nothing. Your name on a letterhead. Later..." I hesitated. "Later it might mean access to resources. Institutional legitimacy. A framework for the work you're doing that isn't just 'rogue professor with unauthorized telescope time.'"

She looked at the forms for a long time. Boltzmann was kneading her lap, claws catching the corduroy.

"You're building something," she said. Not a question.

"Yes."

"And you know exactly what shape it needs to be."

"Yes."

"Because of the foreknowledge."

"Yes."

She signed the advisory board form. Dated it. Handed it back.

"I still don't trust your explanation," she said.

"I know."

"But the data holds up. Every time. And that—" She shook her head. "That's enough for now."

---

The paperwork went out in March. I got the determination letter in June — approved, retroactive to the filing date. I was now the executive director of a 501(c)(3) disaster preparedness nonprofit with zero funding, zero staff, and a three-person board of directors that included my college roommate.

I filed the letter in a manila folder, put the folder in my desk drawer, and sat there for a while looking at the wall.

Every relationship I have is built on a lie I'm telling for the right reasons.

That's what I wrote in my journal that night. Not a physical journal — I'm not an idiot, I don't leave evidence lying around — but a text file on an encrypted USB drive that I kept in a hollowed-out copy of *The Brothers Karamazov* on my bookshelf. Melodramatic? Yes. Effective? Also yes.

*Every relationship I have is built on a lie I'm telling for the right reasons. Marcus thinks I'm passionate about emergency preparedness. Chen thinks I'm a fascinating anomaly to be studied. Frank thinks I've just grown up.*

*None of them know the truth. None of them can. The truth is too big and too absurd and too terrifying, and the moment I tell it, I become the crazy person in the room instead of the prepared one.*

*Is lying for the right reasons better or worse than lying for selfish ones? I used to lie because I wanted things — approval, sex, money, another drink, another hit. Now I lie because I want people to survive. The mechanism is the same. The muscle memory is the same. Open your mouth, shape a story, watch the other person's face to see if they're buying it.*

*I'm good at this. I was always good at this. And that's the part that scares me.*

*The lies I tell now are kinder. They're architecturally sound — load-bearing deceptions that hold up a structure meant to protect people. But they're still lies. And every lie is a wall between me and the person I'm lying to.*

*Chen comes closest to the truth. She has data. She has the signal. She has fifteen months of Friday meetings and a growing file of predictions that keep coming true. But even she doesn't know the real shape of it. Even she doesn't know that I'm not psychic or prophetic or brilliant — I'm just a fuckup from the future who got a second chance he doesn't deserve.*

*Frank said I changed my shape. He's right. But the old shape is still in there, underneath. The liar. The addict. The kid who took everything and gave nothing back.*

*I'm building something good on a foundation of bullshit. I have to believe that the good part matters more than the bullshit part. Because if it doesn't, then I'm just the same person I was before, running the same cons with higher stakes.*

*Anyway. The point is—*

I closed the file. Encrypted the drive. Put it back in Dostoevsky.

---

Summer 2012. NEORAN existed on paper, in a file drawer, and exactly nowhere else. I had a mission statement that I'd revised nineteen times. I had a website I'd built myself — crude, functional, unmistakably the work of a twenty-one-year-old with access to a WordPress template. I had business cards.

What I didn't have was credibility, funding, or any reason anyone should listen to me.

That was about to change. Not because of anything I did — or not entirely. But because in September 2012, I'd put up flyers for a campus disaster-preparedness volunteer event, and a woman I'd never met in my first life would tear one off a bulletin board in the veterinary sciences building and think: *Huh. Thursday works.*

But I didn't know that yet. In the story I was living, as opposed to the story I remembered, the future was still uncertain in all the ways that mattered.

I filed the nonprofit paperwork. The name was boring. Institutional. Exactly the kind of thing the world needs and will never notice until it's far too late.

---

## Chapter 10: The Woman Who Didn't Care About Charm

The flyer was nothing special. I'd designed it in twenty minutes on a library computer, using a template that looked like every other campus event announcement — bold sans-serif header, bullet points, a clip-art icon of a first-aid kit that I immediately regretted but couldn't figure out how to replace.

**DISASTER PREPAREDNESS VOLUNTEER ORIENTATION**
*Learn practical skills. Help your community.*
*Thursday, September 13, 7:00 PM*
*Maucker Union, Elm Room*
*Free pizza. Seriously.*

The "free pizza" was what got most of them. I'd learned that about college students — not just in this life, but in the first one too. You can promise world-changing experience, resume-building opportunities, the satisfaction of civic engagement, and you'll get six people. Promise free pizza and you'll get forty.

I got forty-three. They filed into the Elm Room with the particular energy of undergrads who have been promised food and are slightly suspicious that there's a catch. There was a catch, of course — the catch was me, standing at the front of the room with a PowerPoint I'd built myself and the nervous conviction that I was about to waste everyone's evening.

"Hey. Thanks for coming. I'm Nate Callahan, I run a nonprofit called NEORAN — Northeast Regional Awareness Network — and tonight we're going to talk about what happens when systems fail."

I'd rehearsed this. I'd rehearsed everything. The tone — casual, not preachy. The content — practical, not theoretical. The pizza — ordered from Pizza Pit, arriving at 7:15, timed to hit right when attention started flagging.

The first twenty minutes went fine. I talked about the 2008 Cedar River flood — most of them had been in middle school, but they remembered. I talked about supply chains, infrastructure dependencies, the invisible web of systems that keep a community alive: water treatment, electrical grid, food distribution, medical supply logistics. I kept it concrete. I kept it moving.

And then I asked for questions, and a woman in the third row raised her hand.

She was sitting slightly apart from the others, in a folding chair she'd pulled to the end of the row, as if she'd arrived after everyone else had settled and refused to climb over people to fill a gap.
