Dark hair pulled back in a ponytail — practical, not styled. A flannel shirt over a tank top, jeans, boots that had actually been used for their intended purpose. A nose that had clearly been broken at some point and set slightly off-center, giving her face an asymmetry that made her harder to look away from, not easier.

"Yeah," she said. "You're talking about what happens when systems fail. But you haven't said anything about what you actually do when they do."

Forty-two people turned to look at her. She didn't seem to notice or care.

"That's—" I recalibrated. "That's a fair point. The next section covers response protocols—"

"No, I mean *you*. You, specifically. What have *you* done? When a system failed, what did you do about it?"

This is the moment where charm is supposed to work. I know charm. I've been doing charm since I was fifteen — the self-deprecating smile, the quick pivot, the joke that makes the question disappear. In my first life, charm was my primary survival mechanism. In this one, it's a tool I reach for when I don't want to be honest.

I reached for it. "Well, I personally arm-wrestled a tornado once, but that's a story for after the pizza arrives—"

Nobody laughed. She certainly didn't. She just looked at me with an expression that said: *That's what I thought.*

"Sandbagging," I said, dropping the bit. "2008 and 2010 floods. I've done CERT training, FEMA ICS certification, CPR and advanced first aid. Last spring I helped coordinate volunteer logistics for the county EMA during the derecho warning." I paused. "It's not glamorous."

"I didn't ask if it was glamorous," she said. "I asked if it was real."

"It's real."

She nodded. Sat back. That was apparently sufficient.

I finished the presentation with a low-grade awareness that I'd just been tested and had nearly failed. The pizza arrived. People ate. I circulated, answered questions, handed out signup sheets. Sixty percent of the room put their names down, which was better than I'd expected and approximately the going rate for "free pizza converted to mild obligation."

The woman from row three was at the whiteboard.

I noticed this because no one else was at the whiteboard. The whiteboard was at the back of the room, ostensibly for brainstorming exercises I'd planned for the second half of the orientation but hadn't gotten to because I'd run long on the infrastructure section. It was blank. Or it had been blank.

Now it had a diagram on it. A supply chain diagram. Lines and nodes and labels in small, precise handwriting — the kind of handwriting that belongs to someone who has written a lot of things that needed to be legible under bad conditions.

I walked over. She was still drawing, adding connections with a blue marker, muttering to herself.

"What's this?" I said.

"Your food distribution problem." She didn't look up. "You said the grocery stores in Cedar Falls have three days of inventory on a rolling basis. And the regional distribution center is in Waterloo, which floods. So." She tapped a node. "If the distribution center goes offline, you've got seventy-two hours before shelves are bare, assuming no panic buying. With panic buying, call it thirty-six."

"Right."

"So you need a pre-staged cache system." She drew another node. "But you can't just stockpile — you need rotation, or everything expires. So you tie it to the food bank. Regular donations cycle through the cache, keeping it fresh, and in an emergency, you redirect distribution." She drew an arrow. "Church basements. They have the space, they have the volunteer infrastructure, and nobody's going to loot a church basement."

I stared at the whiteboard. She'd solved in nine minutes a logistics problem I'd been working on for three months.

"And here—" She drew a second cluster. "Veterinary supply chain. People forget about animals. You've got livestock operations north of here, plus companion animals. In a flood evacuation, half your volunteer capacity gets eaten by people refusing to leave without their dogs. So you pre-plan animal sheltering." She added a label. "Fairgrounds. They have stalls, they have fencing, they have loading infrastructure."

"Who are you?" I said.

She looked at me for the first time since I'd walked over. Brown eyes, steady, slightly narrowed — not unfriendly exactly, but not interested in making me comfortable.

"Elena Vasquez," she said. "Pre-vet. Junior."

"Nate Callahan."

"I know. You said that. At the beginning." She turned back to the whiteboard, added another node. "Your electrical grid section was wrong, by the way. You said Cedar Falls has one primary substation. It has two. The second one's on West First Street, serves the industrial district."

"I—" I blinked. "How do you know that?"

"My uncle is an electrician. I spent two summers running conduit for him." She capped the marker. Looked at her diagram with the critical eye of someone who builds things and is never fully satisfied with them. "This needs more work. But the bones are right."

I was staring. I knew I was staring. I made myself stop.

"Do you want to grab coffee?" I said. "After this? To talk about—" I gestured vaguely at the whiteboard.

She looked at me with an expression I would later learn was her default: evaluative, unhurried, completely undazzled. "I have an eight a.m. lab."

"Right. Of course."

"But Thursday works." She pulled a torn strip of paper from her back pocket — the bottom of my flyer, I realized, the part with the date and time — and wrote a phone number on it. Handed it to me. "Text me the details."

Then she took a piece of pizza and left.

---

I stood in the Elm Room for a while after everyone else had gone. The whiteboard was still up — her diagram, her handwriting, her nine-minute demolition of my three-month logistics problem. I took a picture of it with my phone. Then I took another one, in case the first one was blurry.

Here's what I need to explain, and I'm going to do it badly because language wasn't built for this:

In my first life, I pursued women the way I pursued everything else — strategically, selfishly, with an angle. I was good at it. Not because I was attractive or charming in any genuine sense, but because I was observant and adaptable. I could figure out what someone wanted to hear and say it. I could identify the gap between who they were and who they wanted to be, and position myself in that gap. It worked. It worked consistently. And it left behind a trail of women who eventually realized they'd been in a relationship with a performance, not a person.

Elena had no angle. She wasn't performing, wasn't positioning, wasn't trying to be anything other than exactly what she was: a twenty-year-old pre-vet student who thought in systems and had no patience for rhetoric. She'd asked me a direct question, received a bullshit answer, called it bullshit without raising her voice, and then gotten the information she actually wanted. She'd seen a problem, walked to a whiteboard, and solved it. She'd handed me her number not because I'd charmed her but because the work was interesting and Thursday was open.

Every woman I pursued in my first life, I pursued because I saw an angle. Elena has no angle. She's just there, being competent and slightly annoyed, and I can't breathe.

That's what I wrote in my journal that night. Melodramatic. True anyway.

---

Thursday. I texted her the details — same room, seven p.m., smaller group this time, focused on logistics planning. She replied: "Ok." No period, no emoji, no additional words. One syllable. I stared at it for an unreasonable amount of time.

The Thursday group was eight people, all drawn from the original forty-three. The ones who'd actually signed up for the working committee, not just the pizza. Elena arrived at 6:58, took the same position — end of the row, slightly apart — and listened without speaking for the first thirty minutes.

I was trying not to perform. This is harder than it sounds. When you've spent a decade — two decades, if you count both lives — being the guy who talks, who fills silences, who directs attention, turning that off is like trying to stop breathing. You can do it for a few seconds but then your body takes over.

So I was performing. Less than usual, but still. The self-aware version of performance, which is just performance with an extra layer of exhaustion.

Elena raised her hand at the thirty-minute mark. "Can I use the whiteboard?"

"Yeah. Go ahead."

She walked to the front of the room and spent twelve minutes laying out a volunteer coordination system that made my existing plan look like a child's crayon drawing. Tiers of response. Communication trees. Skills-based deployment — not just "show up and we'll tell you what to do" but pre-assigned roles based on training level. She'd clearly been thinking about this since Tuesday.

"Questions?" she said when she was done.

A guy named Trevor, engineering major, raised his hand. "Where did you study this?"

"I didn't study it. I grew up on a ranch. You learn systems or things die."

She sat back down. I realized I hadn't taken a single note because I'd been too busy watching her think.

---

After the meeting, eight people became six — two left for other obligations — and the six of us ended up at the coffeehouse on College Hill. Not my idea. Trevor suggested it, and Elena said "Sure," which I'd already learned was her version of enthusiasm.

I ended up next to her at the long table, not by engineering but by the simple geometry of who sat where. She ordered black coffee. I ordered the same, though I usually took cream, because — I don't know. Because I'm an idiot.

The group talked about logistics for a while. Then the conversation fragmented the way conversations do — side discussions, tangents, people checking their phones. I found myself in a pocket of relative quiet with Elena, who was tearing her napkin into precise strips and stacking them.

"Pre-vet," I said. "What kind?"

"Large animal. Equine, mostly."

"You grew up with horses?"

She glanced at me. The evaluative look again — what are you really asking. "Yeah. Quarter horses. My family ran a breeding operation outside of Clarinda."

"Ran?"

A pause. One strip of napkin, torn. Added to the stack. "Runs. My mom runs it now."

I didn't push. You learn — if you have any sense at all, if you've done any growing — that some silences have doors behind them and the door opens from the inside only.

"The nose," I said instead. Because I'd noticed, and because asking about a visible injury is somehow less intrusive than asking about an invisible one. "Horse?"

She touched it absently. "Three-year-old colt. Threw his head up while I was checking his feet. Broke it in two places." She almost smiled. "My own fault. I was standing wrong."

"How old were you?"

"Fifteen."

"Jesus."

"It's not that dramatic. Horses weigh a thousand pounds and they're basically toddlers. If you work around them long enough, you get hurt. The trick is not getting hurt stupid." She sipped her coffee. "I got hurt smart. Wrong place, right technique. The colt was green and I knew he was green and I positioned myself where his head could reach me. That's on me."

I liked her. I liked her with a clarity and directness that was different from anything I'd felt before — not the acquisitive wanting of my first life, not the calculated pursuit of someone who might fill a role I needed filled. Just: *I like this person. I like how she thinks. I like that she takes responsibility for getting hit in the face by a horse.*

"What about you?" she said. "How does a twenty-one-year-old end up running a disaster nonprofit?"

"Short version or long version?"

"Short. I have an eight a.m. lab, remember?"

"I watched systems fail. Up close. And I thought: somebody should be ready for next time. And then I thought: why not me."

She looked at me for a long moment. Not evaluating this time — or not only. Something else. Like she was checking my answer against some internal standard and finding it acceptable.

"That's honest," she said.

"Mostly."

"What's the part that isn't?"

I laughed. Couldn't help it. "That's — you don't let anything slide, do you?"

"No." Not apologetic. Not confrontational. Just a fact.

"The part that isn't honest," I said, slowly, "is the implication that it was a noble decision. It wasn't. It was—" I searched for a word that was true. "Compulsive. I couldn't not do it. So it's less 'I chose to help people' and more 'I am physically unable to stop trying to prevent things I know are coming.'"

"Things you know are coming," she repeated.

"Climate events. Infrastructure failures. The stuff I was talking about Tuesday."

"You said 'know,' not 'think.'"

Sharp. Uncomfortably sharp. I reached for my coffee, which was getting cold and which I didn't actually like black.

"Everyone knows. Most people just don't look."

She accepted this, or appeared to. Went back to her napkin strips. "My dad used to say that. Not about disasters. About — other things." She looked at the stack of paper. "He'd say: 'Everyone can see it, *mija*. They just don't want to.'"

"He sounds smart."

"He is." Present tense, I noticed. Wherever he was, he was still a living man to her.

She didn't elaborate. I didn't ask. The door stayed closed, and I kept my hands off the handle.

---

We walked out together. The rest of the group had filtered away during the conversation — I hadn't noticed when. The coffeehouse was emptying, chairs being stacked. October night air hit us outside, that specific Iowa autumn cold that isn't bitter yet but carries a warning.

Elena pulled her flannel tighter. "You need better data on the residential side," she said. "You're treating all of Cedar Falls as one unit, but the flood plain and the higher ground are completely different populations with different needs. And you're not accounting for undocumented residents at all."

"I know."

"Do you?" She stopped walking. We were in the parking lot now, under a light that buzzed with that high-frequency fluorescent sound. Her face was half-shadowed. "Because your plan assumes everyone will interface with official systems. They won't. Some people have reasons not to call 911. Your volunteer network needs to reach those people specifically, or you're just running a preparedness club for people who were already going to be fine."

I stared at her. She said it matter-of-factly, like it was obvious, like anyone would have seen it. But no one had seen it. Not me, not Marcus, not the county EMA coordinator I'd been volunteering with for two years. Only Elena, who understood — from the inside — what it meant to be part of a community that official systems didn't protect.

"You're right," I said. "That's a massive gap."

"I know." She started walking again. Then stopped. Turned back. "Same time Thursday?"

"Yeah." I nodded. Tried not to look like a man who'd just been taken apart and reassembled by a conversation about flood logistics. Probably failed. "Yeah. Absolutely."

"Okay." She hitched her bag higher on her shoulder. "Good."

She walked to her car — a beige pickup, old, probably her family's, with a ranch supply sticker on the bumper — and drove away. She didn't look back.

I stood in the parking lot for a full minute. The fluorescent light buzzed overhead. The October air moved around me. Somewhere on campus, music was playing — a party, a dorm room, the normal sounds of twenty-year-olds being twenty-year-olds.

I was grinning. Standing in a parking lot at ten-thirty on a Thursday night, grinning like an absolute idiot, with no strategic explanation for it. No angle. No plan. No calculation of what this could become or how I could use it or what role she might fill in the architecture I was building.

Just: *Thursday. She'll be there Thursday.*

There was no version of my first life where this happened. There was no woman in that life who would have walked to a whiteboard and solved my problems and then asked what I'd actually *done*. In that life, I would have seen Elena Vasquez and thought: *No angle. Not useful. Move on.* And I would have missed — entirely, completely, catastrophically — the only person who was ever going to make me better at this not because she believed in my mission but because she understood, on a structural level, what building something real required.

I walked home. The grin wouldn't go away. I didn't try to make it.

Thursday. She said Thursday.
## Chapter 11: The Letters We Don't Send

The attic smells like mouse droppings and old paper and the particular kind of dust that only accumulates in spaces people avoid. I'm twenty-one years old and I'm helping my mother carry boxes down a pull-down ladder because she decided, on a Tuesday in November, that it was time to "get organized up there." Which means she watched a show about decluttering and felt guilty about the state of things overhead.

"Careful with that one," she says from below, hands raised like she's going to catch a box that weighs thirty pounds. "That's got your grandmother's sewing kit."

I bring it down one-handed, bracing the other against the ladder frame. The box is water-stained on one corner. Inside, wrapped in a flour-sack towel that's older than I am, there's a Singer sewing kit in a leather case, the kind with the fold-out trays and tiny compartments for bobbins.

"She made your christening gown with that," Diane says, opening the case with the reverence people reserve for relics. "Hand-stitched the lace. Took her three months." She touches a spool of white thread, faded to ivory. "You screamed through the whole ceremony. Father Morrissey said you had opinions."

"Still do."

She laughs. It's a real one. That's been happening more often—the real laughs, not the careful ones she used to deploy like shields. I've been sober two years. I've been present. The math is simple: show up consistently and people stop bracing for your absence.

We work through the morning. She tells me about each object like she's narrating a museum tour for an audience of one. Frank's old bowling trophy from the league he quit in '94. Sophie's first pair of shoes, bronze-coated, one of them missing the heel tip. A box of Christmas ornaments Diane's mother painted by hand—cardinals and snow scenes on wooden discs, the paint cracking now along the grain.

"Keep or donate?" I ask about a set of curtains still in their packaging.

"Oh, keep. Those are for the guest room when I redo it."

She's been going to redo the guest room for six years. I don't say that. I just stack the curtains with the keep pile and reach for the next box.

It's up near the eave, shoved behind a garment bag full of bridesmaid dresses from the eighties—teal satin, enormous shoulders. The box is small. Shoebox-sized. No label. I pull it toward me and the top shifts, revealing notebooks. Three of them, the kind with marbled covers you get at the drugstore. I recognize Diane's handwriting on the first page of the top one before I register what I'm reading.

*November 2008*

I should put it back. I know I should put it back.

"Mom, what do you want me to do with—" I lean toward the ladder opening but she's gone. I hear water running in the kitchen. She's making tea. She'll be back in five minutes with two mugs and an opinion about the bridesmaid dresses.

I open the notebook.

Her handwriting is the same rounded cursive she uses for grocery lists and birthday cards, but the lines slant more here, as if she was writing fast or writing angry or writing both. I flip through. It's not a diary in the traditional sense—there are no "Dear Diary" headers, no dates on every page. It's more like a collection of letters she never sent. To Frank. To her sister. To herself.

To me.

I find it near the middle of the second notebook. The date is March 2009. I was nineteen. I was drunk most of the time and lying about the rest.

*I'm losing him. I watch him choose the easy thing every time and I don't know how to stop it. Am I the reason? Did I make it too comfortable to fail? Frank says leave him alone, let him figure it out, but Frank didn't see him last Thursday. I found the bottles under his bed when I was putting laundry away and I just stood there. I just stood there holding his clean shirts and looking at the bottles and I thought: I did this. Somehow I did this. He was such a bright kid. Everyone said so. And now I'm losing him and I don't know which part was the mistake—the loving too much or the not-enough.*

The dust catches the light from the attic's single bulb. Motes hang suspended, moving so slowly they seem frozen. I'm standing with my head bent under the roofline and my chest feels like someone's got a hand inside it, squeezing.

There's more. The next page:

*I read an article about enabling. The signs. I have all of them. Every single one. I make his excuses. I soften his consequences. I tell Frank not to push too hard. What if pushing is what he needs? What if the soft place I keep being is the very thing that's killing him?*

I close the notebook. I place it back in the box. I align the box behind the garment bag, exactly where it was. I adjust the angle so the dust pattern isn't disturbed. These are the skills of a former addict—we know how to make things look untouched. We know how to erase our presence from a scene.

"Nate? Tea's ready!"

"Coming down," I call. My voice sounds normal. That's another skill.

I descend the ladder. Diane hands me a mug—Earl Grey, too much sugar, the way she always makes it. Her face is open and happy. She's enjoying this. The organizing. The togetherness. The son who shows up on a Tuesday to help with boxes.

"Find anything interesting up there?"

"Bunch of bridesmaid dresses. Teal."

She groans. "Oh God. Karen's wedding. 1987. I looked like a teal refrigerator."

"You should keep them. Halloween potential."

She swats my arm. We drink our tea. She tells me about Karen's wedding—the best man who got drunk and fell into the cake, the flower girl who refused to walk and had to be carried. Normal stories. The kind of stories mothers tell their present, sober, accounted-for sons.

I don't tell her I read the journal. I will never tell her I read the journal. She will never know that I know about the guilt she carries—that she thinks she made it too comfortable for me to fail, that she sees my old destruction as her architecture. She's wrong, of course. The drinking was mine. The lying was mine. The choosing easy was mine.

But I can't tell her she's wrong without telling her I read it.

So instead I carry boxes down the ladder and laugh at the teal dresses and drink too-sweet tea and try to be the answer to a letter she never sent.

---

Three weeks later, the ice storm hits.

It starts as freezing rain on a Thursday night in December and doesn't stop for thirty-one hours. By Friday afternoon, half of Black Hawk County is without power. By Saturday morning, it's three counties—Black Hawk, Bremer, Butler. The weight of the ice brings down power lines and tree limbs and, in one case on Highway 218, an entire billboard advertising a personal injury lawyer. The irony writes itself.

I'm in my apartment when it starts. By the time it's clear this isn't a normal winter storm, I'm already on the phone. This is what NEORAN was built for. It's barely operational—we have a filing with the state, a checking account with eight hundred dollars in it, and a website I built myself that looks like it was designed in 2003. But we have a phone tree. We have a list of community centers with backup generators. We have relationships with the county emergency management office because I've been showing up to their meetings for six months, the youngest person in the room by three decades, taking notes.

Elena picks up on the second ring.

"You watching this?" I ask.

"Already lost power at my building. I'm at the library."

"I need you at Covenant Church on College Street. They've got a generator and they just agreed to open as a warming center. Can you get there?"

"Give me twenty minutes."

She doesn't ask why. She doesn't ask what the plan is. She trusts that I have one. That trust is earned—we've been working together for five months now, every Thursday and some weekends, building out NEORAN's volunteer roster and resource maps. She's seen my binder. The binder is impressive in a way that makes normal people uncomfortable.

By Saturday evening I haven't slept. The roads are coated in an inch of ice and the temperature's dropping. We have three warming centers open—Covenant, the VFW on Seerley Boulevard, and the community room at Bartlett Hall that UNI let us use after I spent forty minutes on the phone with the facilities director. Elena is running Covenant. I'm floating between all three, coordinating supply drops and tracking capacity.

The phone trees work. That's the thing—they work. Mrs. Patterson from the emergency management meetings calls twelve seniors on her block. Nine of them need rides. We get them rides. Tom Birch, who I met at the volunteer event where I met Elena, has a truck with chains on the tires. He makes six trips. Everything connects. Every piece we put in place over the past months has a function, and the functions combine into something that actually helps.

I'm at Bartlett at two in the morning, Saturday bleeding into Sunday, when Elena calls.

"We've got forty-three people at Covenant and we're running low on blankets. Also, Mrs. Henderson needs her oxygen. Her backup tanks are at her house on Tremont."

"I'll send Tom."

"Tom's truck died. He's stuck on University Avenue."

I rub my eyes. "Okay. I'll figure it out. How are you?"

"I'm fine. How are you?"

"Fine."

"Nate. When did you last sleep?"

I have to think about it. "Thursday night? I think? What day is it?"

"It's Sunday now. Technically."

"Then Thursday night. But I'm—"

"That's not heroic, that's stupid. Sleep."

"I can't. Mrs. Henderson—"

"I'll handle the oxygen tanks. Jake Morgan has a Subaru with all-wheel drive, he's here. Sleep. I'm not asking."

She hangs up. I stare at the phone. In my experience, people who tell you to sleep don't usually arrange the logistics to make it possible first. Elena does both. She issues the order and removes the obstacle in the same breath.

I find a cot in the supply room at Bartlett. Someone—I don't know who—puts a blanket over me at some point because I wake up three hours later with one draped across my chest that wasn't there when I laid down. My phone has four missed calls. I answer them all in sequence. The ice is still falling.

The storm breaks Sunday afternoon. The power stays out for another two days in most areas, three in some. The warming centers stay open until Tuesday. By the time we close down operations, we've sheltered a hundred and seventeen people, coordinated eighteen supply runs, and facilitated transport for thirty-four elderly residents who would have otherwise been alone in freezing houses.

The county emergency management director, Bill Travers, shakes my hand on Tuesday afternoon outside Covenant Church. "You've got a future in this," he says. He's sixty-two, face like a topographical map of worry.

"That's the plan."

"I mean it. What you built here—the phone trees, the resource lists, the way you had volunteers pre-assigned to roles before the first call went out. That's not amateur hour. That's infrastructure."

I want to tell him that I built it because I know what's coming. Not this storm—this storm was luck, or maybe inevitability, or maybe just Iowa being Iowa in December. But there will be worse. There will be so much worse. And the difference between a community that survives and one that doesn't is exactly this: pre-assigned roles, resource lists, phone trees. The boring architecture of preparedness that nobody funds because it doesn't photograph well.

Instead I say, "Thank you, sir. Means a lot."

Elena is inside, sweeping the church basement. She's been here since Thursday night, same as me. Her hair is in a braid that's come half undone. There's a smear of something—peanut butter, maybe, from the sandwiches—on her jacket.

"Bill Travers just told me I have a future in this," I say.

"No shit." She keeps sweeping. "You also have a present in this. Which involves helping me stack these chairs."

I stack chairs. We work in the particular silence of people who've been through something together—not the silence of nothing-to-say but the silence of not-needing-to. When we finish, the basement is clean and empty and the fluorescent lights buzz in the quiet.

"Thank you," I say. "For all of it. The whole weekend."

She leans the broom against the wall. Looks at me straight on—Elena always looks at you straight on, never sideways, never from angles. It's disorienting if you're used to people who communicate obliquely.

"You don't have to thank me. I volunteered. That's what volunteering means."

"Still."

"Still nothing. But you're welcome." She pulls on her coat. "I'm going home to shower for approximately nine years. Same time Thursday?"

"Same time Thursday."

She leaves. I stand in the clean, empty basement of Covenant Church and I feel something I don't have a word for. Not pride exactly. Not satisfaction. Something closer to recognition—like looking at a blueprint and then looking at the building and seeing that they match. This is what I came back to do. Not just the disaster response, but the being-here. The showing up when the power goes out and the roads are sheets of ice and Mrs. Henderson needs her oxygen tanks. This is what I choose instead of the easy thing.

Anyway. The point is—

---

Marcus calls on a Wednesday in January. It's become routine—Wednesday nights, nine o'clock his time, eight mine. He's in his senior year at Iowa State and he sounds tired in the way that engineering students always sound tired: caffeinated, slightly manic, proud of their suffering.

"So the bridge project," he says. "We've been modeling load distributions for three weeks and Professor Kim keeps finding errors in our finite element analysis. She's relentless. It's like having a very polite assassin review your homework."

"What kind of errors?"

"Boundary conditions, mostly. We keep over-constraining the model and getting artificially stiff results. The real structure would flex more than what we're predicting, which means our safety factors are inflated."

I understand maybe forty percent of this. But I ask questions because Marcus lights up when he talks about it—I can hear it in his voice, the gap-toothed enthusiasm that makes him sound fourteen again, showing me his sketches of impossible buildings.

"There's also a girl," he says, and his tone shifts. Careful. Almost shy.

"Yeah?"

"Her name's Priya. She's in my thermodynamics section. She's—I don't know. She's really smart. And she has this laugh that's like—" He stops. "This is stupid."

"It's not stupid. Tell me about the laugh."

"It's just—she laughs with her whole body. Like the joy goes all the way through her. I've never seen anyone do that."

I'm sitting in my apartment, phone pressed to my ear, and I'm smiling at the wall. Marcus in love is Marcus at his most genuine—no performance, no cool, just wonder.

"Have you asked her out?"

"We've gotten coffee twice. But I don't know if she thinks they're dates or just study sessions. We did study. Both times. Thermodynamics."

"Marcus. You have to tell her."

"I know. I know. I'm working up to it." A pause. "How about you? Anything happening with the volunteer girl?"

"Elena. And no. We're just working together."

"Uh-huh."

"We are."

"Nate. You talk about her every time we're on the phone."

"Because we work together. On the nonprofit. Which is a major part of my life right now."

"Sure. And you describe what she was wearing."

I open my mouth. Close it. He's not wrong. Last week I told him she'd switched from a green jacket to a blue one and it was strange how much it changed her face. That's—okay. That's a data point.

"Anyway," Marcus says, and I can hear the grin. "The point is. Ask her out."

"The point is you should ask Priya out."

"Deal. Same time next week?"

"Same time next week."

I hang up. The apartment is quiet. Three relationships maintained through presence. Marcus on Wednesdays. Elena on Thursdays. Frank and Diane and Sophie on the irregular schedule of family. I used to think relationships required strategy. Turns out they mostly require showing up.

---

The rain starts Thursday night. By Saturday, the river is over flood stage. The sandbagging starts. And somewhere in the middle of all of it—the mud and the water and the exhaustion—something is going to break open.

But that's the next chapter.

For now: Elena, thirty hours into the ice storm response, falling asleep on my shoulder in the Covenant basement. Both of us finally sitting down. She slept for two hours. My arm went completely numb. I didn't move it. I didn't move at all.

She never mentioned it after. I never mentioned it after. Some things live in the space between what happened and what's acknowledged, and they're more real for the silence.

---

## Chapter 12: What Mud Reveals

The Cedar River crests eleven feet above flood stage on a Sunday in April, and the city of Cedar Falls learns what it's made of.

I've been awake since Friday. That's not precisely true—I slept two hours Saturday morning on a cot at the EOC, and Elena made me eat a sandwich at some point that I don't remember consuming but must have because the wrapper was in my pocket later. But functionally I've been running on adrenaline and bad coffee for sixty hours, and my body is starting to register its complaints.

The sandbagging is relentless. We have sixty volunteers across three sites—the levee on First Street where the commercial district meets the river, the residential stretch on Rownd Street where the older houses sit too low, and the park district where the storm drains are backing up. Elena's running Rownd Street. I'm coordinating between all three. Tom Birch is on First Street with a crew of twelve, filling bags from a dump truck full of sand that the county delivered Friday.

My radio crackles. Elena's voice, calm and precise: "North end of Rownd, we need ten more bodies. The water's coming over the top."

"Copy. Sending from the park crew—they've got capacity."

"Also, our sandbag supply is getting low."

"Truck's en route. Twenty minutes."

"Make it fifteen."

I make it fourteen. The truck driver, a county employee named Gerald who's been working since Thursday, gives me a look that says *I am too old for this* and also *I respect whatever insanity you're running here* and peels off toward Rownd Street.

Fourteen hours. That's how long Sunday lasts. Fourteen hours of sandbagging and pumping and evacuating and coordinating and watching the river rise and rise and rise until, around nine p.m., it stops. Holds. The crest comes and it's there and it's bad but it's not catastrophic because we built a wall between the water and the town and the wall held.

Not everywhere. Three houses on Rownd Street took water—the ones south of Elena's perimeter, the ones we couldn't reach in time. A business on First Street, a flooring company with a below-grade loading dock, that one was gone by Saturday night. The park is underwater entirely, playground equipment sticking up like the skeleton of a drowned circus.

But it could have been so much worse. Bill Travers says it, the mayor says it, the local news says it: *it could have been so much worse.*

I know. I know exactly how much worse it could have been.

---

The parking lot behind the Rownd Street staging area is mud. Two inches of it, the thick Iowa kind that sucks at your boots with every step. I'm standing in it. Elena is walking toward me from the staging tent—walking slow, the way people walk when they've been on their feet for fourteen hours and their body has run out of the chemical that makes movement feel effortless.

She's covered in mud. Her jacket, her jeans, her boots, her hands. There's a streak of it across her forehead where she's pushed her hair back. Her braid has come completely undone and her hair hangs loose and wet against her neck. She looks like she's been dragged through a swamp. She looks like the most competent person I've ever known.

She stops in front of me. We're under a parking lot light, the kind that turns everything orange-yellow. The rain has stopped but the air is still thick with moisture, the smell of the river—organic, cold, alive.

"We did it," I say.

"We did it," she says.

And then she steps forward, takes my face in both her muddy hands, and kisses me.

It's not gentle. It's not tentative. It's not a question. Her mouth is warm and tastes like the bad coffee we've all been drinking and her hands are cold on my jaw and she kisses me like she's been thinking about it for a long time and decided there's no reason to pretend otherwise.

When she pulls back—not far, just enough to talk—she says: "I've been waiting for you to do that for six months. You were taking too long."

I'm standing in two inches of mud in a parking lot behind a sandbag staging area and my hands are on her waist because at some point during the kiss they found their way there, and I say the only thing I can think of:

"Six months?"

"At least. Possibly longer. You're not very perceptive." She's still holding my face. Her thumbs are on my cheekbones. "I got tired of waiting."

"I was going to—"

"When? When were you going to?"

"I don't know. Eventually."

"Eventually is too long. I did it now." She lets go of my face. Steps back. Studies me with that straight-on look. "So?"

"So what?"

"So is this happening? Are we doing this?"

"Yes," I say. "God. Yes."

"Good." She nods once, decisive, the same way she nods when we confirm a supply delivery or approve a volunteer schedule. Then she smiles—rare with Elena, and devastating for the rarity—and says, "You look terrible."

"You have mud on your face."

"You have mud everywhere."

"Fair."

We stand in the parking lot. The river holds at crest. The pumps hum. Somewhere on First Street, Tom Birch is probably asleep against a sandbag. The town is battered but intact. And Elena kissed me in the mud after fourteen hours because she got tired of waiting for me to be brave.

---

Tomorrow is not different.

Elena calls me at noon on Monday. "Are we together? Yes or no."

"Yes."

"Good. Don't waste my time."

That's it. That's the entire negotiation. Elena doesn't do ambiguity. She doesn't do the slow approach, the undefined period, the what-are-we phase. She identifies what she wants, confirms it's mutual, and proceeds.

Dating Elena is not like anything I imagined dating would be. She's practical about it. She builds us into her schedule the way she builds everything into her schedule—with intention and without apology. Tuesday evenings and weekends are ours. Thursdays and Saturdays are still NEORAN. She maintains the distinction because she's seen couples who merge their work and personal lives and lose both.

"We need boundaries," she says, two weeks in. "When we're working, we're working. When we're not working, we're not working. Otherwise it all becomes one thing and one thing is fragile."

---

May. Dinner at the Callahans'.

Frank monopolizes her immediately. They're in the garage within fifteen minutes of arrival, looking at his workbench setup. I can hear them through the kitchen window—something about a leaking spigot and whether to replace the whole assembly or just the washer.

"PVC solvent cement on the joint," Elena is saying. "But you'd want to cut below the crack. No point patching over compromised material."

"That's what I said." Frank's voice has the warmth he usually reserves for particularly well-made tools.

I'm at the kitchen counter with Diane.

"She's lovely," Diane says.

"She's my colleague."

"Mmhmm." Diane puts a dish away. "She's lovely and you're watching her through the window like she's on television."

Sophie materializes. "She's way too good for you, by the way."

"I know," I say.

Diane touches my arm. Brief, light. "No she's not."

Dinner is chicken and rice. We sit at the kitchen table—the same table where I ate cereal a thousand times, where Frank told me he was proud, where Diane set down plates for years while I was disappearing. Elena compliments the chicken. Frank asks about the flood response. Sophie corners Elena in the living room afterward—I can't hear what they're talking about but Sophie is leaning forward, which means she's interested.

This is it. This is the life I came back for.

---

June. Elena and I are on the couch in my apartment. Her feet are in my lap. She's reading a large animal anatomy textbook.

"Hey," she says, not looking up. "Can I ask you something?"

"Sure."

"Why disaster prep? Most guys our age are trying to get into finance."

"I think it matters."

"That's not an answer."

She's right. It's not.

"I just—I grew up here. I've seen what happens when storms hit and nobody's ready."

Elena studies me. Weighing my words on her precise scale.

"Okay," she says. "That's part of it. But it's not all of it." She picks up her textbook. "You'll tell me the rest when you're ready."

She returns to her diagrams. I sit with my hand on her ankle and my heart in my throat and the knowledge that Elena Vasquez is smarter than every deflection I've ever built.

---

NEORAN grows. First paid staff member—Jamie Ostrowski, twenty-four, just out of the Army. We apply for a bigger grant. Federal this time—FEMA's Community Preparedness program. $50,000. The application is sixty pages.

The grant comes through in September. Thirty-seven thousand. Enough to hire Jamie full-time and lease dedicated office space.

Elena and I celebrate at the diner on Main Street. Breakfast at nine p.m.—our thing. She eats pancakes. I eat eggs. We're twenty-two and twenty-three and we're building something that matters and we're together and for the first time in either of my lives I feel like the present tense is enough.

---

November. Late. A Tuesday. We're in bed. The room is dark except for streetlight through the blinds.

Elena is on her side, facing away, breathing starting to even out.

"You're going to tell me eventually," she says. Sleep-thick, barely a murmur. "Whatever it is you're not saying."

I don't respond. I stare at the ceiling.

"I'm not in a hurry," she says, quieter still. "But I know there's something. And you're going to tell me. Because that's what this is. This is the kind where you tell each other things."

Her breathing deepens. She's asleep.

She's right. She's already right. She's been right since the parking lot, since she took my face in her hands and closed the distance I was too scared to close.

I'm going to have to tell her. Not everything—maybe not ever everything—but something. Some version of the truth.

But not tonight.

Tonight I lie beside her and listen to her breathe and feel the particular terror of being known. She's pointed at my truth like a compass needle and she's not wavering.

The countdown continues. The storms will come—bigger, worse. And between now and then there's this: a woman asleep beside me who refuses to accept half-truths.

I close my eyes. Elena shifts in her sleep, her hand finding my arm. The radiator ticks.

Anyway. The point is—

She's already asleep. She's already right.
## Chapter 13: Bridges and Thresholds

Marcus shows up on a Friday in July with a six-pack of root beer and a sunburn that makes him look like he fell asleep inside a rotisserie chicken. He's driving a used Civic now, bought with his first real paychecks from Kiewit Engineering, and he parallel parks it on the street outside my apartment with the careful precision of someone who's spent four years solving structural load problems and can't turn it off.

I watch from the window. He gets out, stretches, checks his phone, then looks up at my building like he's assessing its foundation. Old habits.

"You look like a lobster," I tell him at the door.

"Field survey in Ames. Four hours in a hardhat with no sunscreen." He holds up the root beer. "Housewarming gift. Even though you've lived here for a year."

"It's the thought that counts."

"The thought was: Nate probably doesn't have anything good to drink."

"I have water."

"Exactly."

The apartment is small — one bedroom, a kitchen that's really just a counter with ambitions, a living room where I've set up a desk and a whiteboard covered in NEORAN project timelines. Marcus scans the whiteboard the way he always scans things: methodically, left to right, top to bottom. Taking inventory.

"You're busier than last time I was here," he says.

"We got the FEMA partnership." I crack open a root beer and hand it to him. "Three county emergency management offices using our volunteer coordination platform now."

"That's real."

"It's real."

He settles onto my secondhand couch, which protests with a sound like a cat being stepped on. Marcus doesn't weigh much — the couch is just dramatic. He takes a long pull of root beer and looks at me with those steady eyes. Six years I've known this version of Marcus. Six years of weekly phone calls, of him showing up, of the kind of friendship that doesn't require spectacle. He just keeps appearing. Keeps being present. It's the most radical thing anyone's ever done for me, and he doesn't even know it.

"So," he says. "Elena."

"What about her?"

"You've been together almost a year and I still haven't met her properly. The volunteer event doesn't count — there were forty people and a collapsed staging area."

"She's at the clinic until six. You'll meet her at dinner."

"Good." He takes another drink. "Your mom says she's wonderful."

"My mom thinks anyone who's not actively on fire is wonderful."

"Your mom told me Elena talked to your dad about French drains for forty-five minutes."

"She did."

"Nate." Marcus leans forward, gap-toothed grin widening. "She talked to Frank Callahan about drainage. Voluntarily. That woman is a keeper."

I can't argue with that. Elena sat on the Callahan back deck — the deck I built with Frank — and discussed the relative merits of interior versus exterior waterproofing with the intensity of someone defending a thesis. Frank had looked at me afterward with an expression I'd never seen from him before. Something close to relief. Like he'd been waiting to see if I could find someone who'd stay, and here was evidence.

"She is," I say. Just that. No deflection, no joke. Some things don't need armor.

---

Elena joins us for dinner at a Thai place downtown. She's still in scrubs from the vet clinic, her dark hair pulled back, a smudge of something I don't want identified on her collar. She shakes Marcus's hand with the grip of someone who regularly restrains seventy-pound dogs and says, "You're the one who calls every Tuesday."

"Wednesday," Marcus corrects.

"He talks about you every Wednesday night," she tells him. "I time my showers around it."

Marcus laughs — a real one, surprised out of him. Elena does that. She takes people off-guard by being exactly who she is, immediately, without warm-up or pretense.

Over pad thai and spring rolls, I watch them find each other. Marcus talks infrastructure; Elena talks about the time she splinted a horse's leg in a thunderstorm. They discover a shared love of disaster — the managed kind, the kind where you show up and fix things.

"He's not as put-together as he looks," Elena tells Marcus, gesturing at me with a spring roll. "He forgets to eat. He stays up until three working on grant proposals. He runs at five AM regardless."

"He's always been like that," Marcus says. "Minus the running."

"The running is non-negotiable," I say.

"The eating should also be non-negotiable," Elena says.

Marcus is grinning at both of us. The gap in his teeth catching the restaurant light. He looks happy — genuinely happy to see me here, in this life, with this person who argues with me about nutrition.

---

Later. Back at the apartment. Elena's gone to bed — she has a six AM shift — and Marcus and I are on the tiny balcony. It's not the deck I built with Frank, but it overlooks a parking lot and a strip of trees, and if you squint, you can pretend the streetlights are stars.

"She's good for you," Marcus says. Not a question.

"Yeah."

"You're good for her too. In case you were about to deflect."

I was. He knows me well enough to preempt it now.

We sit in the kind of silence that only works between people who've logged enough hours together. The air is heavy with Iowa July — humid, alive, smelling of cut grass and asphalt that hasn't cooled yet.

"You're better than you were," Marcus says eventually. His voice is quiet. Not careful exactly — Marcus is never careful with me, which is why I trust him — but measured. "Way better. But there's a room in you I'm not allowed into."

I don't say anything.

"I'm not asking," he continues. "I'm just saying I see it. There's something you're carrying. You don't have to tell me. But I see it."

The thing about Marcus is that he's never once pushed. Not in six years. He's noted things — my predictions, my certainty about events before they happen, the way I sometimes look at the sky like I'm expecting something. He said it once, years ago: "There's something you're not saying." And I said: "When I can tell you, I will." And he let it sit. He's letting it sit now.

"When I can tell you," I say, "I will."

"I know." He takes a sip of root beer. "That's not what this is about. I'm just saying — whatever it is, it hasn't broken you. Whatever you're carrying, you're carrying it well. That's worth something."

I look at the parking lot. The trees. The streetlights that aren't stars.

"Thank you," I say.

"Don't thank me. Just keep doing whatever you're doing." He pauses. "And eat lunch."

---

The Cedar Falls Planning Commission meets on the third Thursday of every month in a conference room that smells like burned coffee and old carpet. I'm here because NEORAN has community interest in the proposed development along Dry Run Creek. Flood plain management, drainage infrastructure, emergency access routes.

I don't know Derek is involved until he walks in.

He's wearing a suit. Not a college-kid-playing-dress-up suit — a real one, fitted, charcoal, no tie because he's not trying that hard. He looks like money. He looks like confidence that's been professionally laundered.

Now he's at the front of the room, shaking hands with the commission chair, and I realize with a slow sinking feeling that the Dry Run Creek development — the one NEORAN submitted concerns about — is his.

Holt Development Group. I'd read the name on the proposal documents. Hadn't connected it. Derek Holt, right there in the name, and I missed it because I wasn't looking for him.

He sees me. His expression doesn't change — it just gains an additional layer. Recognition. Amusement. Something else.

The meeting proceeds. Derek presents his development plan with the polish of someone who's done this many times. Seventy-two single-family homes. Mixed-use commercial space. He uses phrases like "community-integrated design" and "sustainable growth corridor."

When public comment opens, I stand up.

"Nate Callahan, Northeast Regional Awareness Network. We submitted written comments regarding flood plain encroachment and emergency access limitations for the western parcels."

I lay out the issues: hundred-year flood plain maps, inadequate drainage proposals, single-access road. I cite FEMA data. I reference the 2008 flood. I'm not attacking his project — I'm raising legitimate concerns.

Derek watches me from his seat at the front. His face is perfectly neutral. Attentive.

---

Afterward, in the parking lot, Derek catches up to me.

"Callahan."

"Derek."

"NEORAN." He says it like he's tasting it. "That's yours?"

"Founded it three years ago."

"I know. I looked you up after I saw the comment letter." He tilts his head. "Emergency management nonprofit. Federal partnerships. You've got — what, eight employees?"

"Ten, as of last month."

"Ten." A small smile. "You went from burnout to nonprofit director. That's a hell of a rebrand."

"It's not a rebrand."

"Everything's a rebrand. Every version of ourselves is a rebrand." He says it without malice. Like it's obvious. Like the idea of genuine transformation is a charming fantasy he's too sophisticated to believe in.

"I disagree," I say. "But we don't need to have that argument in a parking lot."

"No, we don't." He looks at me for a long moment. "You want to grab a drink? Talk about this like adults?"

"Sure," I say. "There's a place on Main. I'm buying."

---

The bar is dim and half-empty. Derek orders bourbon, neat. I order club soda with lime. He notices but doesn't comment.

We talk for an hour. About his business — three developments completed, two more in planning. About NEORAN — the growth, the challenges. He asks good questions. Derek's intelligence was never the problem. His ethics were the problem.

"Here's what I'll do," Derek says finally. "I'll hire a real drainage firm. Do it right. Submit a revised plan. It'll cost me four months and probably eighty grand, but it'll be a better project."

"Why?"

"Because you're right and I'd rather spend eighty grand than fight you at meetings for half a year." He slides a card across the table. "Also because I want you to owe me one."

"That's not how it works."

"Everything's how it works if you frame it right."

We stand. At the door, he stops. Turns back to me.

"You know what I like about you, Callahan?" His voice is casual. Almost friendly. "You're the only person I've met who's as strategic as me but pretends not to be."

He walks to his car — something German and black and expensive — and drives away without waiting for a response.

I stand in the doorway with his card in my hand. The night air is warm. Someone's laughing on the street a block away.

The worst part — the part that's going to keep me up tonight — is that Derek isn't wrong.

I am strategic. I've always been strategic. The foreknowledge just gave me something worthy to aim it at. Without it, who would I be? Without the countdown, would I be the one in the tailored suit, cutting corners because profit is the only game that makes sense in a world without meaning?

I don't know. And not knowing is the room Marcus isn't allowed into. Not the foreknowledge itself — but the question of whether I'm good or just well-aimed.

Anyway. The point is—

I drive home. Elena is asleep. I stand in the kitchen for a long time, looking at the whiteboard, and I think about bridges.

Eight years. Maybe nine. The countdown doesn't care about my moral philosophy.

---

## Chapter 14: The Sealed Envelope

Chen's office smells like coffee and cat. Boltzmann is asleep on a stack of papers that are probably someone's dissertation. The afternoon sun cuts through the blinds in horizontal lines across Chen's desk, across her face as she looks at me with the expression I've come to recognize: she is about to demand something.

"I need more," she says.

"More what?"

"More data. More specifics." She takes off her glasses and rubs the bridge of her nose. "Nate, we've been working together since 2011. I need something I can verify independently. Something with a timeline."

It's July 2015. I'm twenty-four.

"What would satisfy you?" I ask.

"Something astronomical. Something I can observe. Something with a specific time, specific coordinates, that hasn't been predicted by anyone else."

I've been thinking about this. The Pathfinder objects will enter the outer solar system around 2018. Three years from now. Detectable with the right instruments.

"Objects," I say. "Plural. Entering the outer solar system. Around 2018."

Chen goes very still. Boltzmann yawns in his sleep.

"What kind of objects?"

"Artificial. Not natural. Moving on trajectories that aren't consistent with any known orbital mechanics."

"You're talking about..." She doesn't finish.

"I'm talking about contact. Not landing on the White House lawn. Something slower. Something that starts at the edge of the solar system and takes years to resolve. But it starts in 2018."

She stands up. Sits back down. Walks to the window.

"If this is real," she says to the window. "If what you're telling me is real—"

"I know."

"No, you don't." She turns back. "I'm the one who has to decide whether to believe you. I need it documented. Written. Sealed. Time-stamped."

"That's why I'm here."

---

We do it that afternoon. Chen provides university letterhead. I write by hand:

*Objects of artificial origin will enter the detectable range of the outer solar system (beyond Neptune's orbit) between January and June 2018. Approximate approach vector: ecliptic plane, bearing consistent with origin from galactic center direction. Multiple objects. Non-ballistic trajectory corrections will be observable.*

*Written July 14, 2015. Nate Callahan.*

Chen reads it three times. Her lips move.

"Approach vector from the galactic center direction," she repeats. "That's specific."

"It's correct."

She takes the paper. Folds it carefully. Puts it in an envelope. Writes the date. Signs across the seal. Walks to the combination safe behind her desk. The lock clicks. The envelope disappears. The safe closes.

"Three years," Chen says.

"Three years."

"I'm going to need more coffee."

---

The months after become a blur of growth. NEORAN expands — ten employees by early 2016. Elena leaves her vet clinic to come on as operations coordinator. Marcus chairs the technical advisory board. Regional offices in Waterloo, then Dubuque. Federal money flows.

Elena and I move in together fall 2016. A two-bedroom apartment. The second bedroom becomes an office Elena immediately colonizes with veterinary journals and color-coded binders.

She organizes by season. I cook badly. We fight about dishes and reconcile over takeout. We get a dog — a rescue mutt she names Hazard. One ear up, one ear down. He eats three leashes in the first month.

Sophie comes for dinner in April 2017. She's twenty-one. Less prickly. More curious.

"You really did change," she says. "I still don't understand how."

"I had good reasons."

"You still won't tell me what they are."

---

Thanksgiving 2017. The Callahan house.

Frank carves the turkey. Diane orchestrates. Marcus and Elena debate disaster infrastructure — levees versus community preparedness. Sophie argues with everyone. Hazard is under the table performing structural analysis on shoelaces.

I look at this table. Frank and Diane. Sophie. Marcus. Elena. This improbable collection of people who love me. This life I built piece by piece from the rubble of what I was.

I have eight years left.

Frank catches my eye across the table. Nods. The nod that means: this is good, bud. This is right.

I pick up my fork. I eat Diane's turkey. I laugh at Sophie's jokes. I am here, fully here, and I am also counting. I am always counting.

---

New Year's Eve, 2017. Our apartment roof.

It's cold. December-in-Iowa cold. Elena is bundled in a coat that makes her look like a small puffy astronaut. Between us, a thermos of hot chocolate. Below, Cedar Falls prepares for midnight.

But I'm looking at the sky.

I can't help it. Beyond the stratosphere, beyond the Kuiper belt — the objects that Chen and I sealed in an envelope two years ago. Drifting inward. Getting closer. Still invisible, but there. Moving.

Three months. Maybe four. Before the first confirmed observations.

"Hey," Elena says.

I look at her. She's watching me with that expression — the one that means she's seeing something I'm not showing.

"What do you want this year?" she asks.

"More time," I say.

Elena smiles. She thinks I mean us — more time together, more mornings and arguments and the dog pressed between us on the couch. She thinks it's romantic.

And I do mean that. I mean every second of this. I want decades of it.

But I also mean everything else.

More time before the objects arrive. More time before the world learns. More time to build NEORAN into something strong enough. More time to prepare the people I love for something I can't yet explain.

"More time," I say again.

Elena reaches across and takes my hand. Below us, someone sets off a premature firework — a single red burst that fades into smoke.

"We've got plenty," she says.

I don't contradict her. The fireworks begin. Colors bloom overhead, reflected in her eyes. The year turns. 2018 begins.

Seven years left. Maybe.

More time.

She thinks I mean us.

I do.

I also mean everything else.
## Chapter 15: The Envelope Opens

The call comes at 11:47 PM on a Tuesday in March, which is how I know it's real before I even look at the screen. Good news arrives during business hours. Interesting news arrives over dinner. The kind of news that rearranges the architecture of your life — that comes close to midnight, when the world is thin.

Elena shifts beside me. Hazard lifts his head from his spot at the foot of the bed, ears forward.

"Callahan," I say, because I've never learned to answer the phone like a normal person.

"Nate." Chen's voice. I've known her three years now, and I have never heard her sound like this. There's a vibration underneath the word, like a tuning fork struck against bone. "Nate, I need you to come to my office."

I'm already sitting up, feet on the cold floor. "Now?"

"Now. Right now."

Elena props herself on one elbow. "Work?"

"Chen," I say. "Something's happened."

"Drive safe," she says. "It's supposed to ice over."

I kiss her forehead. She's already falling back to sleep, or pretending to. Hazard settles his chin on her ankle.

The drive takes thirty-two minutes at this hour. Empty roads, the heater cranking against March cold, my headlights cutting through fog. I drive ten over the limit because my hands are shaking.

I have been waiting for this call for three years.

---

Her door is open. The light spilling into the hallway is blue-white — her monitors. She's standing in front of them when I round the corner, and she turns, and her face does something I can't read. Fear, maybe. Wonder.

"Close the door," she says.

I close it. Boltzmann watches me from his perch on the filing cabinet.

"Sit down."

I sit. Chen crosses to her desk, picks up a manila envelope — the envelope, my handwriting on the front, the notarized seal — and holds it up.

"I opened this two hours ago," she says. "After I received confirmation from three separate observatories."

She sets it on the desk between us. Next to it: printouts. Trajectory plots. Spectral analysis.

"Four objects," she says. "Entering from beyond the Kuiper Belt, trajectories consistent with deceleration profiles that shouldn't exist in natural bodies. Albedo readings that suggest manufactured surfaces. Three observatories confirmed independently within six hours."

She picks up the envelope. My handwriting on the back: *Objects consistent with manufactured origin will be detected entering the outer solar system. Expected trajectory: 40-60 AU, decelerating. Detection window: 2017-2019.*

"The detection window," she says. "The trajectory. The spectral anomaly." She sets it down like it weighs more than paper. "You predicted this three years ago."

"I know."

"Who are you?"

The question that has sat underneath every conversation since the beginning.

"I'm exactly who I've always told you I am," I say. "The prediction isn't fraud, and it isn't luck."

"Then what is it?"

I lean forward. "What do you want me to say, Judith?"

"The truth. Which you have been parceling out to me like I'm a child who can't handle it."

I stand up. Walk to her window. The campus is dark below.

"The objects are precursors," I say. "Scouts. They're the first stage of a larger event."

"What kind of event?"

"An arrival. Not human. It's not like the movies. It's indifferent. They're not coming for us. We're just... in the way."

"In the way of what?"

"Resources. They strip biospheres. Atmosphere, water, organic material. Systematic. Efficient." I turn back to face her. She's gripping the edge of her desk. "Like strip mining. On a planetary scale."

"How do you know this?"

"Because I was there. I saw it. I can't explain how. But I experienced what comes after those objects arrive. The timeline. The stages. All of it."

She stares at me for a long time. Boltzmann jumps into her lap and she pets him absently.

"You're describing temporal displacement," she says finally.

"I don't know what it is. I know what happened to me."

"The precision of your prediction is inconsistent with delusion."

"Yes."

"A delusional person might predict alien contact. They would not predict specific detection parameters three years in advance."

"No. They wouldn't."

Silence. Boltzmann purrs. The heater ticks.

"How long?" she asks. "From detection to what you described."

"About eight years. Give or take."

She processes this. Chen doesn't panic — she reorganizes information into structures she can work with.

"What are the stages?"

"The scouts map. Then a gap — years. Atmospheric changes start around 2023. Subtle. Then 2025, escalation. And in 2026—"

"Eight years," she says. "That's what you're saying."

"Seven and change."

She sits down. Boltzmann adjusts. "Then we'd better get to work."

---

The paper goes up on arXiv three weeks later. *Anomalous Trans-Neptunian Objects: Preliminary Observations and Trajectory Analysis.* The response is what we expected — a handful of interest, a larger handful of dismissal. Chen's reputation takes a hit. Not fatal. But bruising.

She doesn't stop. She secures more observation time. Publishes follow-ups. Slowly, carefully, a few more people start paying attention.

Meanwhile, NEORAN scales. Des Moines. Omaha. Kansas City. I hire a deputy director. I travel. I build. The infrastructure grows, piece by piece, community by community, boring and institutional and exactly what the world will need.

Elena notices the hours. The calls at midnight. The drive to campus at 2 AM. She notices everything. She always has.

"You're home less," she says one Sunday morning. Hazard between us on the couch.

"NEORAN's expanding."

"I know what NEORAN's doing. I'm asking about you."

"I'm fine. Just busy."

She looks at me over her reading glasses. The look says: I know that's not the whole truth.

"Okay," she says. And goes back to her book.

I should tell her. I think that every day. But I don't. Because I'm afraid. Because the last time I told someone the complete truth, Chen asked "Who are you?" with horror in her voice. And Elena is not a scientist. Elena is the woman who shares my bed and if she looks at me that way—

I can't. I'm not brave enough.

---

## Chapter 16: The Box Under the Bed

It happens on a Tuesday in October. October 15th, 2019.

I am in Des Moines when she calls. A planning meeting. My phone lights up: Elena. I almost let it go to voicemail. Something makes me pick up.

"Hey."

Silence. Not the silence of a dropped call — I can hear her breathing. Hazard's nails clicking on the floor.

"Elena?"

"Come home." Her voice is level. Absolutely level. Like a table saw running.

"I'm in Des Moines. I won't be back until—"

"Come home now, Nate."

My chest goes cold.

"Are you okay? Is Hazard—"

"The dog is fine. Come home."

She hangs up.

The drive is an hour and a half. Ninety on the highway. My mind running through possibilities — break-in, injury, emergency — and not once do I think about the box under the bed.

I forgot about it. Not forgot — suppressed. It became furniture.

---

The apartment is quiet. Hazard meets me whining, his whole body doing that anxious wiggle.

"Elena?"

"Bedroom."

I walk down the hallway. My feet are heavy.

She's sitting on the bed. Cross-legged. The box is open beside her — white cardboard banker's box. The contents spread across the comforter: envelopes, printouts, newspaper clippings, a black notebook.

Her face is the worst part. Not angry. Not crying. Careful. The expression of someone handling unstable materials.

"Close the door," she says.

I close it. Hazard settles against the wood.

"I was looking for the tax documents," she says. "The 2018 returns."

She holds up an envelope. My handwriting. The date. The notary stamp.

"This describes objects entering the solar system. Written in 2015." She sets it down. Picks up a newspaper clipping — 2008, the financial crisis. My handwriting in the margin: a date, three weeks before the article was published.

She picks up the notebook. Opens it.

"2023 — atmospheric seeding begins. 2025 — escalation. June 2026 — arrival." She reads it flat. No inflection. "What is this?"

I open my mouth.

"The truth," she says. "Nate. The truth."

And I panic. The only reflex that fires is the oldest one.

"It's a creative writing project."

The words come out before I can stop them. They sound wrong. Flat, tinny. A creative writing project. As though the notarized envelopes and coded correspondence are worldbuilding.

Elena looks at me. She doesn't blink.

"A creative writing project," she repeats.

"Elena—"

"You are telling me it's fiction."

I can't speak.

"I have watched you," she says. She stands. "For three years, I have watched you build a disaster preparedness organization with the intensity of a man trying to prevent his own death. I have watched you take calls at midnight. I have watched you come home with that look on your face. I have watched you and I have not pushed because I thought you would tell me when you were ready."

Her voice doesn't crack. Each word is placed with the precision of a scalpel.

"And you're telling me it's fiction."

She crosses to the closet. Opens it. Her overnight bag — sitting on the shelf. Packed. Zipped. Ready. Not hastily assembled. Packed deliberately. With forethought.

She was never going to accept that answer. She called me to see if I would tell the truth. I failed.

"Elena, please. Sit down. Let me—"

"Let you what? Let you tell me another version?" The first crack. Not in her voice but in her eyes. "Either you are deeply unwell in a way I've missed for three years, or you know something that you have chosen — actively, every single day — not to share with me."

"I'm not sick," I say.

She nods. "Then you've been lying to me. For three years." Her voice drops. Quieter. Almost gentle. "You built your whole life around this thing you believe is coming and you didn't tell the person sleeping next to you every night."

"I was trying to protect—"

"Don't. That's not protection. That's control. That's you deciding what I can and can't handle."

She's right. Every word is a blade that finds the exact place I'm softest.

"I need time," she says. She walks past me. I don't move. She passes close enough that I can smell her shampoo — rosemary.

She kneels by Hazard in the hallway. Puts her face against his neck.

"I'll come back for my things," she says. "Later this week."

The door opens. The door closes.

The lock catches with a sound like a small bone breaking.

---

The apartment is very quiet.

I stand in the bedroom doorway for a long time. The light shifts — afternoon to evening. The box sits on the bed, contents splayed. My secrets laid out like a body on a table.

I look at the evidence of my life's project. From the outside, without context — it looks insane. The collected work of a mind that has come unmoored.

Hazard noses the door open. Clicks across the floor. Puts his head in my lap.

"Yeah," I say to him. "I know."

---

I don't drink. The neural pathway that once connected pain to alcohol has been rerouted so many times it doesn't fire anymore. I think about it the way you think about a language you used to speak.

What I do is: nothing. I sit in the dark and I let the grief be what it is.

Around nine, my phone buzzes. Marcus.

*Hey man. You good? You were supposed to call tonight.*

*Bad day. Rain check.*

*Everything ok with Elena?*

*We're taking some space.*

*Call me if you need anything. I mean it.*

I put the phone face-down.

---

She comes back Thursday. I'm at work — probably deliberate. When I get home, the closet is thinner. Her bathroom shelf is bare. The small things gone — books, photos, spare leash.

She left her key on the counter.

No note.

Hazard investigates the closet. Sniffs where her shoes were. Comes back with that look — confused concern.

I pick up the key. Close my hand around it. The metal is cold.

And I go to work the next day. And the day after. Because the countdown doesn't pause for grief. The Pathfinders don't care that I'm alone now.

NEORAN needs me. Chen needs me. The work needs me. So I do the work, and I come home to an apartment that's slightly too empty and a dog who's slightly too quiet.

I sit on the bed some nights and think about the box underneath me and Elena's face — that careful, surgical calm — and I think:

People don't change. They just get better at hiding.

Sophie was right. She was always right.

Hazard whines at the door. I don't know what he's waiting for. Maybe he just knows that the apartment used to have one more heartbeat in it, and now it doesn't.

I sit on the bed. The box is underneath me. Seven years. Less. The world moves toward something, and I just lost the only person who made the preparation feel like anything other than grief management.

I turn off the light. Hazard jumps up and settles in Elena's spot.

I close my eyes. I don't sleep.

The apartment holds its silence around me like something I earned.
The apartment is quieter than it should be.

Not silent. Hazard's nails click on the hardwood when he follows me from room to room, which he does now, constantly, like he's assigned himself a shift and the shift is me. Gerald sits on the back of the couch and watches the front door with the unblinking focus of a creature conducting an experiment. He watched it for three days straight after Elena left. On the fourth day, he moved to the kitchen counter and knocked her coffee mug off the edge. The blue one. The one with the chip in the handle that her mother gave her. It shattered on the tile and I swept it up and threw it away and then stood there with my hands on the counter for a long time because I understood, in some wordless way, that the cat had just decided she wasn't coming back.

That was two weeks ago. Or three. The days have a quality now. They blur at the edges, run together like watercolors left in the rain. I go to work. I come home. The space between is a smear of competence that requires no participation from whatever part of me used to be here.

I go to work. I come home. I feed the animals. I run the numbers on a supply chain contract for a municipality outside Cleveland. I answer Chen's emails with the minimum number of words that still qualify as communication. I eat something, usually standing over the sink, usually cold, usually whatever requires the least number of steps between the refrigerator and my mouth. I sleep on my side of the bed, which is a stupid thing to do when the whole bed is empty, but the body has its habits and the body is the last organ to receive bad news.

Her shampoo is still in the shower. I haven't moved it. I haven't opened it to smell it either. I'm not that far gone. But I know it's there the way you know a bruise is there — you don't have to press it to remember.

The closet is half-empty. She came Saturday while I was out, as she asked. Took her clothes, her books from the nightstand, the framed photo of her parents from the dresser. Left everything else. The apartment looks like a diagram of absence — the shapes of what's missing more visible than what remains.

---

It's a Tuesday when I go to the bar.

Not my bar. I don't have a bar anymore, haven't had one in years. The last bar I went to was in Iowa City in 2011, a night I don't remember clearly, which is its own indictment. This is just a place on Ninth Street with a green neon sign that says CASEY'S in a font that was probably stylish in 1974. I've walked past it a hundred times on the way to the hardware store. Tonight I walk in.

The reason is simple and stupid: the apartment is too quiet and the silence has a texture tonight, something thick and woolly that sits on my chest like a physical weight, and I need to be around noise that isn't mine. I need to be in a room where people are talking to each other and nobody is talking to me and I can exist as a shape on a stool without anyone asking if I'm okay. That's it. That's the whole reason.

I tell myself this as I push through the door. I tell myself this as I sit at the far end of the bar, away from the two guys watching a Cavs game on the mounted TV. I tell myself this as the bartender walks toward me with the easy unhurried gait of a woman who has walked toward ten thousand men on ten thousand Tuesday nights and can sort them into categories before she reaches the taps.

She's maybe fifty. Short gray hair, reading glasses pushed up on her forehead like she was doing the crossword in a back booth before I came in. She looks at me the way bartenders look at everyone — a quick assessment, a sorting. Type of guy. Type of night. What does this one need.

She sets a glass on a napkin and pours two fingers of whiskey without asking. I must look like the kind of man who drinks whiskey on a Tuesday. She's not wrong. I was that man for a long time. In another timeline, I still am.

"Thanks," I say, which is reflex, the way you thank someone for handing you a grenade. She nods and moves down the bar.

The glass sits in front of me.

It's bourbon. I can tell by the color — darker than Scotch, more red in the amber. The overhead light catches it and there's a whole sunset trapped in there, all the warm tones, all the gold. I know without tasting it that it's mid-shelf. Not the well stuff but nothing fancy. The bartender made a judgment call on me and landed in the middle and she's right about that too.

I put my hands on the bar. The wood is scarred and slightly sticky in the way of all bars that have been bars for a long time. Someone carved initials near my left thumb. J.R. + somebody, but the second set of initials has been sanded down, either by time or intention. One half of a declaration that outlived whatever it was declaring.

The glass sits in front of me.

It's warm in here. The heating system is working too hard, the way it does in old buildings with old radiators that nobody's serviced since the Clinton administration. One of the guys at the other end laughs at something on the screen. The Cavs are losing. I don't know the score. I don't care about the score. I'm looking at the glass.

I could pick it up. It would be the easiest thing I've done in weeks. Easier than sleeping. Easier than answering the phone when Sophie calls with her specific questions and her careful concern. Easier than walking past the half-empty closet every morning and not opening the door to check if something has changed, as if closets refill themselves overnight, as if absence is a temporary condition that corrects without intervention.

I pick up the glass.

The weight of it. Two fingers of bourbon and a rocks glass and it weighs almost nothing. It weighs everything. It's warm from the room, body temperature almost, like something alive, and the smell rises — corn and oak and something like vanilla, something sweet hiding beneath the burn. I know what the burn feels like. The way it lands in the back of the throat first, then spreads downward. The warmth in the chest that isn't warmth, that's a lie the nervous system tells itself, but it's a good lie. The best lie. Better than any lie I ever told another person.
