He waves a hand. The one without the IV. "I'm fine. Look at me. I'm fine."

I look at him. He's fine. He's not fine. He's a sixty-four-year-old man in a hospital bed with a piece of metal in his artery and I can see the fear underneath the bluster — just a flicker, just a shadow in the back of his eyes — because Frank Callahan does not get scared, except when he does, except when his body reminds him that it has a vote.

"The doctor says you need to change your diet."

"The doctor's twelve years old."

"She's a board-certified cardiologist."

"With the face of a child."

"Dad."

"I'll eat the rabbit food." He shifts. Winces slightly. "Don't tell your mother I winced."

"I won't."

"Good." A pause. He looks at me — really looks, the way he does sometimes when he thinks I'm not paying attention. "You drove fast."

"Yeah."

"Don't do that."

"Too late."

"Next time, drive the speed limit. I'll still be here when you arrive."

The word *next time* sits between us. An acknowledgment: this might happen again. Bodies do this. Hearts do this. They pause. Sometimes they resume. Sometimes they don't.

"Okay," I say.

"Okay." He reaches out. Pats my hand twice. Gruff. Quick. "Good kid."

I'm thirty-three years old. I'm a good kid.

---

Elena arrives at 6:30. She's brought sandwiches from the deli near the highway — turkey for me, roast beef for Sophie, egg salad for Diane. Nothing for Frank because he's on a restricted diet and Elena reads every instruction.

"How is he?" she asks, handing me the bag.

"Annoyed and alive."

"Best combination."

She sits with us. Eats. Talks to Diane about garden tomatoes — a conversation so mundane and normal that it functions as medicine. Sophie leans against my shoulder and falls asleep for twenty minutes. The hospital dims around us.

At 8 PM, visiting hours end. We say goodnight. Frank is already half asleep, the television playing some game show he's not watching.

"Tomorrow," I say from the doorway.

"Bud." His eyes are closed. "Go home. Pet the dog."

---

I drive home at the speed limit. Elena follows in her car. At home, Hazard is frantic — circling, whining, pressing his nose against my legs.

"It's okay," I tell him. "He's okay."

Dogs don't understand words, but they understand tone. He settles. Follows me to the couch and puts his head on my knee and sighs the way only dogs can sigh — with their whole body.

Elena sits beside me. Close. Her hand on my thigh.

"You okay?" she asks.

"No."

"Okay."

We sit. The house is quiet. Outside, fireflies. The last of the season.

"In the first life," I say. And stop. Because I don't talk about the first life much. Not the details. Not the small, sharp ones.

"Tell me," she says.

"He died at sixty-three. Alone. In his recliner. And I was in Waterloo and I was drunk and I didn't — I couldn't—"

"But that's not this."

"No."

"This is the one where Diane called 911. This is the one where you drove eighty-seven miles an hour—"

"You don't know how fast I was driving."

"I know you." She presses closer. "He's alive, Nate. He's alive because of choices. His. Diane's. Yours. This is the version where people stay."

Hazard's tail thumps once against the carpet. Agreement.

---

Frank comes home three days later. He's on medication — blood thinners, a statin, something for blood pressure. Diane has reorganized the kitchen: olive oil instead of butter, whole grains instead of white bread, vegetables in the crisper that Frank regards with suspicion.

"What's this?" he says, holding up a purple vegetable.

"Eggplant," Diane says.

"Why?"

"Because Dr. Reeves said—"

"The child doctor."

"Frank."

He puts the eggplant down. Looks at it like it has personally wronged him. But he eats it that night — roasted with garlic and herbs, because Diane is a good cook and even eggplant can be tolerable in the right hands.

I come for dinner twice a week. Tuesday and Sunday. Elena comes Sunday. Sophie comes whenever she can. We fill the house with noise and bodies and Frank sits at the head of the table and complains about the food and asks for seconds and I think: *This. This is what I came back for.*

Not the shelters. Not the seed vaults. Not the atmospheric data or the satellite tracking or the forty-three sites across twelve states. Those matter. Those will save lives. But this — this table, these people, this man cutting eggplant with a suspicious fork — this is what I came back for.

---

Fall. The leaves turn. NEORAN activates Phase Three protocols — full communication network testing. Sophie runs it. Three days of rolling drills across all forty-three sites. Satellite uplinks confirmed. Ham radio networks confirmed. Hardened fiber confirmed.

"Ninety-six percent response rate within target window," Sophie reports. "Four sites had equipment issues. Already addressed."

"Good."

"Also, Dad called during the drill. He wants to know if we're coming for Thanksgiving."

"Obviously."

"He also wants to know what NEORAN actually does."

I pause. "What did you tell him?"

"Disaster preparedness nonprofit. Same as always."

"And he bought it?"

"He's never bought it, Nate. He just doesn't push."

She's right. Frank has never pushed. Not about the nonprofit that grew too fast. Not about the late nights. Not about the conversations that go quiet when he walks into the room. He watches. He waits. He trusts that his son will tell him when it's time.

And it's almost time.

---

Winter. December through February — the cold months, the planning months. Chen publishes her second paper. This one is bolder: *On the Anomalous Deceleration of Seven Outer Solar System Objects: Implications for Non-Natural Origin.* Co-authored with Nakamura and Reeves at NOAA.

The Post journalist finally gets through. The article runs on page six of the science section. The headline is cautious: *Physicists Observe Unusual Behavior in Deep Space Objects.* But the data is there. Anyone who reads carefully can see it.

No one reads carefully. The story gets twelve hundred shares on social media. A few comments about aliens. A few jokes. Then it's gone, buried under the news cycle.

"They'll pay attention eventually," Chen says.

"When?"

"When it's obvious."

"By then—"

"I know." She takes off her glasses. Rubs her eyes. "I know."

---

March. Frank's six-month follow-up. The stent is holding. Labs are good. Blood pressure down. Weight down eight pounds — the eggplant is working.

"Clean bill," Frank says, calling me from the hospital parking lot. "Your mother can stop hovering."

"She'll never stop hovering."

"No." A pause. Fond. "No, she won't."

He sounds good. Strong. The fear has faded — not gone, never fully gone after your heart reminds you it can stop — but faded. He's walking every morning. Two miles around the neighborhood. Hazard goes with him sometimes, when I bring the dog over, and they make a pair: the retired pipe fitter and the aging mutt, both of them slower than they used to be, both of them still moving.

I watch them from the kitchen window one morning. Frank talking to the dog, Hazard's tail wagging, the spring light coming in sideways through the budding trees.

Anyway. The point is—

No. Not yet.

The point is that my father is alive. And I'm going to tell him everything.

---

April. I call Sophie.

"I'm going to tell Dad."

Silence. Then: "Tell him what, exactly?"

"Everything. The first timeline. The displacement. Why NEORAN exists. What's coming."

"Nate." A breath. "He's sixty-four. He just had a heart attack."

"He's recovered. And he deserves the truth."

"I'm not arguing he doesn't deserve it. I'm asking if this is the right time."

"There's no right time. There's only the time you choose."

Sophie is quiet for a long moment. I can hear her thinking — the particular quality of her silence when she's running scenarios.

"Do you want me there?"

"No. Just me and him."

"Okay." Another pause. "Be gentle."

"I will."

"He's tougher than we think, you know."

"I know."

"But also. Be gentle."

"Yeah."

---

I pick the day of his follow-up appointment. The six-month check. He'll be at the hospital anyway. Good news from the doctor — stent holding, labs clean. He'll be in a good mood. Relaxed. And I'll be there, because I always drive him now. Since the heart attack, I drive him to every appointment. Frank complains about this. Frank also never says no.

The appointment is April 17th.

I spend the two weeks before drafting what I'll say. Writing it down, crossing it out, starting over. There's no script for this. No playbook for: *Dad, I lived another life before this one. I was a different person. A worse person. You died and I wasn't there and I came back and I stayed.*

But the words don't have to be perfect. They just have to be true.

April 17th. Morning. Clear sky. I drive to Frank's house. He's waiting on the porch, coffee in hand, wearing the flannel jacket Diane bought him last Christmas.

"You're early," he says.

"Traffic."

"There's no traffic between here and your house."

"Got me."

He gets in. We drive. The radio plays classic rock — Tom Petty, which Frank tolerates. Thirty minutes to Mercy General. We talk about nothing: the Hawkeyes' chances next season, the leak in the garage roof, whether Hazard is getting fat.

"He's not fat," I say. "He's sturdy."

"He's round, bud."

"He's a lab mix. They're supposed to be—"

"Round?"

"Sturdy."

Frank laughs. It's a good sound. A sound I want to bottle and keep.

---

The appointment goes well. Dr. Reeves — the child doctor, as Frank insists — reviews his labs, checks the stent imaging, pronounces him in excellent shape for a man of his age and history.

"Keep doing what you're doing," she says. "Walking. Diet. Medication."

"The eggplant?"

"Is it working?"

"Apparently."

"Then yes. The eggplant."

Frank shakes her hand. We walk out. Through the lobby, past the gift shop, through the automatic doors into the late afternoon.

The parking lot is half empty. Late in the day — most appointments done. The light is going golden. That particular spring evening light that makes everything look like a photograph.

Frank's truck is in the far row. We walk toward it. He's moving well — no shortness of breath, no hesitation. Just a man walking to his truck after a good doctor's visit.

He pulls out his keys. Unlocks the door. Doesn't get in. Leans against it instead, the way he does — arms crossed, looking out at nothing in particular.

"Good news," he says.

"Yeah."

"Your mother'll be relieved."

"She will."

He's still leaning. Still looking. And something in the way he's standing — patient, unhurried, present — tells me: now. This is the moment. He's been waiting.

"Dad."

"Yeah, bud."

"I need to tell you something."

He looks at me. Steady. Those pale blue eyes that don't miss anything, that have been watching me for eighteen years, that saw me change from a drinking teenager to whatever I am now and never asked why. Never pushed. Just watched.

"Okay," he says.

And I tell him everything.
## Chapter 22: The Parking Lot

I start wrong. I start with the science.

"There are objects approaching Earth," I say. "Seven of them. They're decelerating — which means they have propulsion. Which means they're not natural. Dr. Chen has published the data. Other researchers have confirmed it. The timeline is approximately eighteen months."

Frank listens. Doesn't move. Arms still crossed, leaning against his truck.

"NEORAN — the nonprofit, the shelters, everything Sophie and I have been building — it's preparation for this. For what happens when they arrive."

He nods. Once. Processing. His face is unreadable.

And I realize — this isn't the thing I need to tell him. This is the easy part. Aliens. Invasion. Data. Chen's papers. These are facts. Facts are simple.

The hard part is the other thing. The thing underneath.

"That's not all," I say.

"Okay."

"Dad, I need you to — I know this is going to sound—"

"Just say it, bud."

The parking lot is quiet. A car pulls out somewhere behind us. The evening light slants long and gold across the asphalt. I can hear birds — starlings, maybe, in the trees along the hospital perimeter.

"I lived another life before this one."

The words land in the air between us. Solid. Irretrievable. I watch Frank's face for the flinch, the doubt, the calculation that his son has lost his mind. It doesn't come. He just watches me. Waits.

"I don't know how. I don't have a mechanism. Chen doesn't either. But I was — I lived to thirty-five. In that life, I was a different person. I drank. I drifted. I burned everything. Everyone." I stop. Breathe. "And then I was seventeen again. Back in my room. Back at the beginning. With everything I knew."

Frank uncrosses his arms. Puts his hands in his jacket pockets. His eyes haven't left mine.

"That was eighteen years ago," I say. "I was seventeen and I remembered being thirty-five and I decided — I decided to do it differently."

"Differently how?" Frank's voice is quiet. Not skeptical. Just asking.

"Everything. School. Work. You. Mom. Sophie." I swallow. "In the first life, I failed at all of it. I dropped out. I drank. I moved away. I didn't come home for years."

The muscle in Frank's jaw moves. Just slightly.

"You and I — we barely spoke. After I left. There were years where I didn't call. Years where you called and I didn't pick up. And then—" My voice cracks. I let it. "And then you died. At sixty-three."

Frank is very still.

"Your heart. Like now. Like what just happened. But in that version, no one was home. Mom was at Aunt Carol's. You were alone in the recliner. And by the time they found you—"

"Nate."

"I was in Waterloo. I was drunk. I flew home for the funeral in a suit that didn't fit and Sophie wouldn't look at me and I held Mom's hand and I thought—" I stop. Because the tears are here now. Not dramatic. Just steady. Rolling down in the late light. "I thought: I built this. Not the heart attack. But the distance. The years of silence. I built all of that."

Frank doesn't speak. He takes his hands out of his pockets. He's looking at the ground now. At the asphalt. At the painted lines of the parking space.

"So I came back. I don't know how. I don't know why. But I came back and I was seventeen and you were forty-seven and you were alive and I thought — okay. Okay. This time I stay."

The silence stretches. A minute. Two. The parking lot lights haven't come on yet — it's that in-between time when the sun is below the treeline but the sensors haven't triggered. Everything is blue-gold. Suspended.

"The drinking," Frank says finally. "In high school. You stopped."

"Yes."

"Overnight. I remember. One week you were — and the next week you weren't."

"That's when it happened. The displacement."

Frank nods slowly. Not agreeing. Processing. Filing.

"And the nonprofit. NEORAN."

"Built for this. For what's coming."

"And Chen."

"She has the data. She believes the data. The time travel part — she sets aside."

"Smart woman."

"Yeah."

Another silence. Frank shifts his weight against the truck. The metal creaks slightly.

"In the first version," he says. And stops. Works his jaw. Tries again. "In the first version. We didn't—"

"We barely spoke. After I was twenty. Fifteen years of almost nothing."

"And I died—"

"At sixty-three. Alone."

"Was I—" He stops again. I see his throat work. "Was I a bad father? In that version?"

The question hits me like a fist.

"No." The word comes out broken. "No, Dad. You were the same. You were exactly the same. You tried. You called. You showed up. I was the one who—" I press the heel of my hand against my eyes. "You were fine. I was the problem. Every time."

Frank is quiet for a long time after that. The parking lot lights come on — sudden, harsh, turning everything flat and white. Neither of us moves.

"So you came back," he says. His voice is rough. Lower than usual. "And you — what. Fixed things?"

"I tried. I stayed in school. Got sober. Went to college. Came home. Started NEORAN. Stayed close." I look at him. "Stayed."

"Eighteen years."

"Yeah."

"You've known for eighteen years. About — all of it. The objects. The first life. And you just—"

"Stayed. Yeah."

Frank turns. Looks out across the parking lot. The lights make his face stark — shadows under his eyes, the lines deep, the jaw set the way it sets when he's feeling something he won't name.

"Why didn't you tell me before?"

I consider the question. The honest answer.

"Because I was afraid you'd think I was crazy. Because the world hasn't ended yet and I have no proof that isn't data and equations. Because—" I stop. "Because what if you looked at me differently. What if you looked at me and saw — a stranger. Someone from somewhere else. Instead of your son."

Frank turns back. Looks at me. The look lasts a long time. Long enough for another car to pull out of the lot. Long enough for the starlings to resettle in the trees. Long enough for the weight of two lifetimes to fill the space between us.

"Nate," he says. And his voice has something in it I've never heard before. Something that sounds like it's being carried up from very deep. "You spent eighteen years—"

He stops. Starts again.

"You could have gone anywhere. Done anything. You had — what, the future? The stock market? All of it?"

"Yes."

"And you came here. And you stayed."

"Yes."

"You fixed your life. You built something. You come to dinner every Sunday." He's looking at me and his eyes are bright and I realize — Frank Callahan is crying. Not openly. Not dramatically. Just a brightness at the edges. A slight catch in his breath. This man who built a deck with a broken finger. Who drove three hours in a snowstorm for Sophie's dance recital. Who says "bud" instead of "I love you" because the words are too big for his mouth.

"You stayed," he says again.

"Yeah, Dad."

"Well."

One word. It takes all the air out of the parking lot.

*Well.*

Like: I hear you. Like: I believe you. Like: Eighteen years of watching you become this person, and now I know why, and it doesn't change anything because you're still my son. Like: I am proud of you in a way I don't have language for. Like: You came back from somewhere impossible and you chose this family and this town and this life and you stayed and that is enough. That is everything.

*Well.*

The parking lot lights buzz. The asphalt is blue-white under them. The evening has gone full dark and neither of us has moved.

"How'd I die?" Frank asks. Quiet. Not afraid. Just — curious. The way you'd ask about a road you didn't take.

"In your recliner. The brown one."

"The La-Z-Boy."

"Yeah."

"Diane hated that chair."

"She still hates it."

"But she hasn't thrown it out."

"No."

Frank makes a sound. Not quite a laugh. "Good chair."

I laugh. Wet and broken and real. "Yeah. Good chair."

Silence again. Comfortable now. The worst has been said. The truth is out — all of it, the time travel and the first life and the drinking and the funeral in the bad suit and the fifteen years of not calling. It's all in the air between us and Frank is still here. Still leaning against his truck. Still looking at me like I'm his kid.

"Your mother," he says.

"Yeah."

"She should know?"

"I — yeah. If you think—"

"Not tonight." He straightens up. Rolls his shoulders. "Tonight she'll have dinner ready and she'll want to hear about the appointment. Good news. Clean labs. Let's give her that tonight."

"Okay."

"But soon."

"Yeah. Soon."

He pulls his keys out. Looks at them in his hand. Looks at me.

"You want me to drive?"

He always drives. His truck, his rules. But he's offering. Something has shifted.

"I'll drive," I say.

He nods. Walks around to the passenger side. Gets in. I get in the driver's seat. Adjust the mirrors. The truck smells like him — coffee and sawdust and Old Spice. It's always smelled like this. In both lifetimes.

I start the engine. Put it in reverse. And then Frank's hand lands on my forearm. Brief. Firm. The same hand that taught me to throw a football, that clapped my shoulder at graduation, that patted my hand in the hospital room three months ago. One squeeze.

"Bud."

"Yeah."

"You did good."

Three words. Thirty-three years in both directions. A whole life of trying — two whole lives of trying — and those three words land like they've been waiting all this time.

I pull out of the parking space. We drive home. The radio plays Tom Petty — "Wildflowers," which isn't classic rock, technically, but Frank likes it anyway. He hums along. Off-key. Comfortable.

We don't talk the rest of the drive. We don't need to.

---

At the house, Diane has dinner ready. Pot roast. The real kind — Frank's cheat day, because good lab results deserve celebration. She takes one look at Frank's face, then mine, and her eyes narrow.

"What happened?"

"Good news," Frank says. "Everything clean." He kisses her cheek. Hangs his jacket. "Where's the pot roast?"

She watches him go. Then turns to me. That mother-look. The one that sees through walls.

"Nate?"

"It's fine, Mom. We just — had a talk."

"What kind of talk?"

"A good one."

She studies me a moment longer. Then nods. Lets it go. Because Diane Callahan also knows when not to push. It's a family trait.

Dinner is normal. Frank eats two helpings. Diane reminds him about portion control. He ignores her lovingly. I sit at the table and eat pot roast and I think about how in the first timeline, I never came to this table after I was twenty. Fifteen years of pot roasts I missed. Sunday dinners I didn't attend. This table, these chairs, this light — I missed all of it. And now I haven't missed it. Not a single one.

After dinner, I help Frank with the dishes. Side by side at the sink. Him washing, me drying. The rhythm is old and comfortable.

"Thursday," he says, low enough that Diane can't hear from the living room.

"What about Thursday?"

"Come by Thursday. We'll tell your mother."

"You sure?"

He hands me a plate. "She's tougher than both of us combined."

"That's true."

"Thursday."

"Okay."

He washes the last dish. Drains the sink. Dries his hands on the towel that's always hung on the oven handle.

"For what it's worth," he says. "I'm glad you came back."

I put the plate away. Close the cabinet.

"Me too, Dad."

He nods. Once. Done. The conversation is over in the way Frank's conversations end — not with a period but with a settling, like a house that's finished shifting on its foundation.

He goes to the living room. Sits in the La-Z-Boy — the brown one, the one he died in once, in another version of this story. Turns on the television. Some documentary about the Great Lakes.

I lean in the kitchen doorway and watch him. Alive. Present. Watching television in his recliner.

"Bud," he says without looking up. "You're staring."

"Sorry."

"Go home. Pet the dog. Tell Elena I said hi."

"Okay."

"Drive the speed limit."

"I will."

He glances at me. A smile — small, private, ours. "Good kid."

I drive home at the speed limit. Every single mile.

---

Thursday. Frank and I tell Diane. Together. In the kitchen, over coffee, at 10 AM.

She listens. Doesn't interrupt. Her hands are wrapped around her mug and they don't shake and her eyes don't leave my face.

When I finish, she's quiet for exactly fourteen seconds. I count.

Then she says: "Well. That explains the socks."

"The — what?"

"The socks. When you were seventeen. One day you didn't care what you wore. The next day you were sorting your laundry and folding your socks like a thirty-year-old." She takes a sip of coffee. "I told your father. I said, 'Something happened to that boy.' And Frank said, 'He's figuring it out.' And I said, 'Overnight? No one figures it out overnight, Frank.'"

She sets her mug down.

"I've been waiting eighteen years for this conversation."

Frank, beside me, makes a sound. "You never said—"

"I didn't know what it was. I just knew something changed." She looks at me. Through me. Into whatever's behind me. "My son went to bed one person and woke up another. A better person. A kinder person. But different." She reaches across the table. Takes my hand. "I've been watching you be different for eighteen years, sweetheart. I'm just glad to finally know why."

I don't cry. I already cried in the parking lot. But I hold her hand and I think: of course. Of course she knew. Mothers always know.

"The invasion part," she says. "That I'll need some time with."

"Take all the time you need."

"I don't have all the time, apparently." She picks her mug back up. Sips. "How long?"

"About a year. Maybe fourteen months."

"And NEORAN is ready?"

"Getting there."

"What do you need?"

I blink. "What?"

"What do you need. From us. To be ready."

Frank nods beside me. Solid. Ready. This is what Callahans do. We get the bad news. We process it. We ask: what do we need to do.

"I need you to be at the Iowa City shelter when it happens. You and Dad. That's all."

"Done."

"And — Sunday dinners. Until then. Every one."

She smiles. Small. Fierce. "Obviously."

---

I drive home. Call Elena.

"They know."

"Both of them?"

"Both. Dad on Tuesday. Mom today."

"And?"

"Dad said 'well.' Mom said she knew something changed when I was seventeen."

Elena is quiet a moment. Then: "Of course she did."

"That's what I said."

"Because she's a mom, Nate."

"Yeah."

"Are you okay?"

I think about it. Really think. Am I okay? The two people who made me know the truth. The full truth — not just the mission, not just the data, but the other life. The first life. The one where I was nothing. They know that person existed. They know I was him. And they're still here.

"Yeah," I say. "I'm okay."

"Come home. Hazard misses you."

"Hazard saw me three hours ago."

"He has a short memory and a big heart. Come home."

I come home.
## Chapter 23: The Ones Who Stay

Marcus comes over on a Saturday in June. He doesn't know why I've asked him. He thinks it's about NEORAN — maybe the new shelter site in Omaha, or the seed vault partnership that's been stalling. He brings beer. Sets it on the counter.

"So," he says. "What's the fire?"

"Sit down."

He looks at me. Something in my face must register because he doesn't argue. Just pulls out a chair at the kitchen table and sits.

Elena is upstairs. She offered to stay. I told her no — this is between me and Marcus. But I know she's up there, probably pretending to read, actually listening through the floor. Hazard is asleep under the table. His breathing is steady. Grounding.

"I need to tell you something," I say. "And I need you to hear all of it before you respond."

"Okay." Marcus leans back. Crosses his arms. The posture of a man who's heard a lot of pitches. "Go."

So I go.

I start at the beginning — the real beginning. Not college, not NEORAN, not the blackmail. The first life. The drinking. The dead-end jobs. Waterloo. The years of nothing. Frank's funeral. The displacement.

Marcus's face doesn't change for the first ten minutes. He's an engineer. He listens to complex systems all day. He collects data before reacting.

But at the fifteen-minute mark — around the part where I explain waking up at seventeen with thirty-five years of memories — his jaw tightens. And when I get to the part about knowing the future, about the stock predictions and the atmospheric data and the objects approaching Earth, he uncrosses his arms.

"Wait," he says.

"I asked you to hear all of it."

"The — you're saying time travel."

"I'm saying I lived another life. I came back. I don't have a mechanism."

"That's not—" He stops. Presses his lips together. "Keep going."

I keep going. NEORAN's real purpose. Chen's confirmation of the data. The trajectory projections. The deceleration. The objects that steer.

When I finish, Marcus is very still. His beer is untouched. His hands are flat on the table.

"How long," he says, "have you known me?"

"Since freshman year. In both timelines."

"And in the first one?"

I take a breath. "You gave me twelve hundred dollars. Senior year of college. I was behind on rent. You put the money in an envelope on the kitchen counter with a key."

Marcus's eyes flicker. Something.

"I never paid you back. I moved out that summer. I left the key on the counter with a note that said 'Sorry.' And we never spoke again."

The kitchen is very quiet. Hazard shifts under the table. Snores.

"The twelve hundred," Marcus says slowly. "You know about the twelve hundred."

"I was there."

"I never told anyone about that. Not — anyone."

"I know."

His hands flex on the table. Flat. Then fists. Then flat again. "And the blackmail. In — what was it, 2019?"

"I needed you to stay. I'd lost you once. I couldn't—" I stop. Because this is the ugly part. The part where I was wrong. "I blackmailed a city council member — Aldiss — to get infrastructure permits through. Used information about his affair as leverage. It worked. It was the worst thing I've done in this life."

Marcus nods. Slow. Not forgiving — acknowledging. He knew about the blackmail — he'd confronted me about it years ago. He stayed despite it. But now he knows the why behind the permits.

"You blackmailed a guy to get your permits."

"Yes."

"Because in the other version—"

"You left. And I deserved it. And this time I was terrified of it happening again and I did a terrible thing to prevent it."

"You could have just told me the truth."

"I was twenty-three. I was terrified. I wasn't ready."

Marcus picks up his beer. Puts it back down without drinking. Stands up. Walks to the window. Looks out at the backyard — the garden, the shed, the fence I built two summers ago.

"I need evidence," he says.

"I have it."

"Not your word. Data. The papers. Chen's number. Cross-references. The trajectory data."

"All of it. Whatever you need."

He turns. His face is — complicated. Not angry. Not disbelieving. Something harder to name. Something that looks like a man standing at the edge of a cliff, deciding whether the ground below is real.

"Give me everything. And I'm going to verify. Everything."

"Take as long as you need."

"I'll need days."

"I know."

He nods. Once. Walks to the table. Picks up his beer. Looks at it.

"Does this one still count?" he asks. "Or is future-beer different somehow?"

A laugh breaks out of me. Surprised and grateful. "It still counts."

He cracks it open. Drinks. Sets it down.

"I'm going to go. I'll take the documents. And I'm going to verify every single data point. And if it checks out—" He stops. "And if it doesn't—"

"Then I'm delusional and you can drive me to a psychiatrist."

"Deal."

He picks up the folder I've prepared — thick, organized, tabbed. Chen's papers. Trajectory data. Atmospheric readings. Prediction logs with dates. My notebooks.

At the door, he pauses.

"The key on the counter," he says. "The note that said 'sorry.' In the first version. What did I do? When I found it?"

"I don't know. I was already gone."

He stands in the doorway. His face does something I can't read.

"I would have been angry," he says.

"Yeah."

"I would have hated you."

"Probably."

"And you spent eighteen years making sure that didn't happen."

It's not a question. He doesn't wait for an answer. He leaves. I hear his car start. The crunch of gravel. Then nothing.

Elena comes downstairs four minutes later.

"How'd it go?"

"He took the documents. He's going to verify."

"And then?"

"I don't know."

She sits beside me. Hazard crawls out from under the table and puts his head on her foot.

"He'll come back," she says.

"How do you know?"

"Because he's Marcus. And you're his best friend. And the evidence will check out." She pauses. "Also because he left his other four beers in the fridge."

---

Five days. Marcus takes five days.

I don't call him. Don't text. Don't drive past his apartment. I give him the space he needs because I've learned — the hard way, in another life — that some people need to reach their own conclusions. You can't push someone into belief.

Day one, Chen calls me. "Your friend Marcus called. He's very thorough."

"What did you tell him?"

"The data. He asked good questions. I gave good answers."

"Did he believe you?"

"He believed the data. That's all that matters."

Day two, nothing.

Day three, Sophie texts: *Marcus called me. Asked about NEORAN operational details. I told him everything. He sounded calm.*

Day four, nothing.

Day five. Saturday evening. 7 PM. The doorbell rings.

I open it. Marcus is standing on the porch. He's wearing a wrinkled dress shirt and jeans. He looks like he hasn't slept well. There's a legal pad in his hand — the yellow kind, covered in notes.

"Can I come in?"

"Yeah."

He comes in. Sits at the kitchen table. Same chair as before. Puts the legal pad down. Looks at me.

"I called Chen," he says. "Twice. She walked me through the trajectory mathematics. The deceleration data is real. Objects in vacuum don't decelerate without thrust. That's— that's physics."

"Yeah."

"I cross-referenced your prediction logs against historical records. Seventeen out of twenty verifiable predictions accurate to within a week. Three were off by small margins — days, mostly. One was wrong entirely."

"The Senate vote in 2018."

"Yeah. You predicted it would fail. It passed."

"Things change. When you interfere with a timeline, things shift."

Marcus nods. Makes a note. "I talked to Sophie. She walked me through NEORAN's infrastructure. Forty-three sites. Twelve states. Operational protocols. She knows everything."

"She knows most of it. Not the time travel part."

He looks up. "She doesn't know?"

"She knows what's coming. Not how I know."

"But Frank does."

"Yeah."

"And Diane."

"Yeah."

Marcus sets his pen down. Leans back. Runs both hands through his hair — a gesture I've seen a thousand times, the one that means he's arrived at a conclusion and is about to say it.

"You had eighteen years of future knowledge," he says. "You knew what was going to happen. Markets. Elections. Technology. Everything."

"Yes."

"You could have made a billion dollars."

"Yes."

"You could have — I don't know — been president. Predicted 9/11. Prevented wars."

"I thought about all of it. Early on."

"And you spent them—" He gestures around the kitchen. At the house. At Hazard asleep on the floor. At the window where you can see the neighbor's fence. "You spent them fixing your relationship with your dad. And building a disaster nonprofit. In Iowa."

"Yes."

"Why?"

The question hangs. Simple. The simplest question.

"Because the first time around, I was rich in nothing that mattered."

Marcus is quiet. He picks up the pen. Puts it down. Picks it up again.

"The twelve hundred thing," he says. "The key on the counter."

"Yeah."

"In the first version. I left a key and you left a note and that was it."

"That was it."

"And you spent eighteen years making sure that didn't happen."

"Yeah."

His eyes go bright. Not tears — Marcus doesn't cry. But a brightness. A heat behind the eyes that says the truth has landed somewhere deep. Somewhere structural.

"I verified for five days," he says. "I called your physicist. I cross-referenced data. I went through seventeen predictions line by line."

"And?"

"And the evidence is overwhelming. The data is real. The deceleration is real. Something is coming."

"Yeah."

"And the time travel—"

"You don't have to believe that part. Chen doesn't."

"I don't need to believe it," Marcus says. "I just need to believe you. And I've known you for twelve years in this version and you've never lied to me. Except the blackmail. The Aldiss thing."

"Yeah."

"Which you've explained. And apologized for. Repeatedly."

"Yeah."

He picks up the legal pad. Flips to a clean page. Takes the cap off his pen.

"Okay," he says. "So. Eighteen months."

"Approximately."

"And NEORAN is at — what. Seventy percent readiness?"

"Something like that. Eighty on shelters. Sixty on communications. Fifty on food production."

"Then we have work to do." He starts writing. Lists. Columns. The engineer's brain clicking into gear. "Food production is the gap. Sixty percent won't sustain the population numbers you're projecting. We need—"

"Marcus."

He looks up.

"You're staying."

It's not a question. But he answers anyway.

"I'm staying." He clicks the pen. "You had eighteen years of future knowledge, and you spent them becoming a guy who shows up." He shakes his head. Slight. Amazed. "Of course I'm staying."

---

The summer accelerates. Marcus embeds in NEORAN full-time — takes unpaid leave from his engineering firm. Within two weeks, he's redesigned the hydroponic yield projections. Within a month, he's identified sixteen structural vulnerabilities in the shelter network that nobody else caught.

"Your ventilation specs are wrong," he tells Sophie on his first day in the office.

"Excuse me?"

"The airflow calculations assume standard atmospheric conditions. If there's particulate contamination — which is what Chen's data suggests — you need three times the filtration capacity."

Sophie looks at me. Back at Marcus. "Who is this person?"

"He's your new head of infrastructure."

"I didn't agree to—"

"Yes you did."

She glares. But by the end of the week, they're working side by side. Sophie on communications and logistics. Marcus on structural engineering and life support. Two competent people who speak the same language of spreadsheets and deadlines and contingency planning.

---

July. Full-network test.

All forty-three sites. Simultaneous activation. Communications, power systems, water recycling, air filtration. Sophie coordinates from the grain elevator office. Marcus monitors structural telemetry from a laptop balanced on a filing cabinet.

I watch. That's mostly what I do now — watch. The machine I built has outgrown me. It runs on the competence of people who are better at their specific jobs than I ever could be. Sophie. Marcus. The site coordinators. Chen, on satellite link, monitoring atmospheric data in real time.

"All sites reporting," Sophie announces. "Thirty-nine green. Four yellow — minor issues. Zero red."

"Which four?" Marcus asks.

"Duluth, Boise, Little Rock, and Austin. Duluth is a communication lag — probably the satellite realignment. Boise has a water pressure issue. Little Rock and Austin are backup generator delays."

"None of those are structural."

"No."

"Then we're good."

"We're good."

I stand in the corner of the office and watch them work and I think: this is what it looks like. Eighteen years of building, and this is what it looks like. Not me saving the world. Not one person with future knowledge making heroic choices. Just a network. Just people. Just competence and trust and the simple, radical act of showing up.

---

August. Chen's third paper drops. This one doesn't need a science section. It makes the front page.

*Seven Objects Confirmed on Earth-Approach Trajectory: International Team Publishes Deceleration Data.*

The world notices.

The President makes a statement. Measured. Calm. The language of managed disclosure: "anomalous objects," "continued monitoring," "no immediate threat." But the data is public now. Anyone with a physics degree can run the numbers. Anyone with a calculator can project the arrival window.

Fourteen months.

The news cycle does what it does. Panic. Then denial. Then memes. Then panel discussions. Then a strange settling — the way a river finds its level after a flood. People adjust. They go to work. They buy groceries. They pick up their kids. Because what else can you do? The objects are far away and abstract and the rent is still due on the first.

"They're handling it better than I expected," Elena says, watching the news.

"It's far away. It doesn't feel real yet."

"When will it feel real?"

"When they're visible. Without a telescope."

"How long?"

"Three months out. Maybe four."

She nods. Turns off the television. "Then we have time."

"Some."

"Come to bed."

---

September. NEORAN goes from forty-three sites to sixty-one. The announcement from the President triggered something — state governments reaching out, FEMA partnerships, NGOs with resources looking for people who've been planning for this. We were already here. We were ready.

Marcus handles the expansion. Sophie handles the comms. I handle — what? The vision. The why. Meetings where I sit at the head of the table and explain what NEORAN does and why and someone inevitably asks: "How did you know? How were you ready?"

And I say: "I've been planning for this for a long time."

Which is the truest thing I've ever said.

---

October. Elena and I are in the living room. Late. Hazard between us on the couch — too old and dignified to sleep on the floor anymore. Elena has her laptop open. She's coordinating with veterinary networks across the Midwest — animal triage, livestock protocols, pet evacuation plans. Things no one else thought about.

"There are sixteen million dogs in the affected radius," she says.

"I know."

"No one has a plan for them."

"You do."

She looks at me. Over the top of her laptop. That look — the one that says: *I see what you're doing and I love you for it but also we have a problem.*

"I have a draft," she says. "I need NEORAN resources."

"Name them."

"Transport. Storage. Supply chains for food and medication."

"Done."

"You didn't even look at the numbers."

"I trust your numbers."

She studies me. Then smiles. Small, warm, private. "You've gotten better at that."

"At what?"

"Trusting people."

She's right. In the first life, I trusted no one. Couldn't. Trust requires being present, and I was never present. Always half somewhere else — the bar, the next town, the next excuse.

Now I trust. Elena. Marcus. Sophie. Frank and Diane. Chen. The sixty-one site coordinators. The network. The people.

It's the best thing I've ever built. And I barely built it. I just showed up, and they built it around me.

---

November. Thanksgiving. Frank's house.

The whole family. Diane's pot roast — Thanksgiving edition, which means turkey AND pot roast because Frank refuses to choose. Sophie and her boyfriend, a quiet electrician named David who installs solar arrays for NEORAN and blushes when Sophie talks about him. Elena beside me. Marcus across the table — invited for the first time, because he has no family nearby and because he's earned his place.

Frank carves the turkey. He's good at it — precise, methodical, the same way he used to cut pipe. His hands are steady. His color is good. The stent holds.

"Marcus," Frank says, passing a plate. "Sophie says you fixed the ventilation."

"It was a calculation error. Not hard to fix."

"Takes a smart person to find a calculation error."

"Takes a smart person to build the system in the first place." Marcus glances at me.

Frank follows the glance. Nods. The smallest acknowledgment — a triangle of knowing. Frank, Marcus, me. The three people at this table who know everything. Diane knows too, of course. And Elena. But the men have their own geometry. Their own quiet understanding.

"Good turkey," Marcus says.

"Diane's recipe," Frank says.

"My mother's recipe," Diane corrects. "Forty years running."

"Forty-one," Frank says. "We've been married thirty-seven but you made it the year before."

"You remember that?"

"I remember everything you cook."

Diane shakes her head. But she's smiling.

I sit at this table and I eat turkey and pot roast and mashed potatoes and I listen to my family talk about nothing important and I think: in fourteen months, the world changes. In fourteen months, everything I've built gets tested. In fourteen months, these objects arrive and whatever they bring with them — destruction, contact, war, something I can't imagine — it comes.

But tonight, there's turkey. And my father is alive. And my mother is strong. And my sister is competent and fierce. And my best friend is here. And the woman I love is beside me, her hand on my knee under the table, warm and present.

This is what I built. Not the shelters. Not the seed vaults. This.

---

December through March. The longest winter.

The objects are visible now. Not to the naked eye — not yet. But amateur astronomers with decent telescopes can find them. Seven points of light, moving. Decelerating. Coming.

The government ramps up. FEMA activates. National Guard deployments to major metropolitan areas. Shelter programs. Evacuation planning. All the things that should have started years ago, starting now.

NEORAN integrates. Sophie is on calls with FEMA coordinators daily. Marcus reviews structural assessments for government shelter sites and finds — predictably — that they're inadequate.

"They built for earthquakes," he says. "Not for prolonged atmospheric contamination."

"Can they retrofit?"

"In fourteen months? Some of them. Maybe half."

"Then we focus on the half."

"Already on it."

---

Chen is on satellite link now. Full-time monitoring. The data streams in constantly — velocity, trajectory, thermal signatures. The objects are not just decelerating. They're maneuvering. Subtle course corrections. Formation maintenance.

"They're coordinating," Chen tells me, 2 AM on a Tuesday. Her face on the laptop screen is tired. Boltzmann is asleep behind her. "Seven objects maintaining formation geometry. That's not gravity. That's communication."

"What kind of formation?"

"Hexagonal. Six around one. The center object is larger."

"Can you determine size?"

"Working on it. Preliminary estimates — the outer objects are approximately three kilometers in diameter. The center object is eight."

Eight kilometers. A city, flying through space. Decelerating toward Earth.

"Chen. What do you think they want?"

She takes off her glasses. Rubs her eyes. "I'm a physicist, Nate. I can tell you what they're doing. I can't tell you why."

"In the first timeline—"

"In the first timeline, did they communicate?"

"I don't know. Infrastructure collapsed before arrival. Everything I know about the actual event is fragments. Fire. Power failures. Then nothing."

"Then we prepare for the worst and hope for less."

"That's been the plan for eighteen years."

"It's a good plan." She puts her glasses back on. "Go to sleep, Nate. I'll call if anything changes."

---

April. Spring. The one-year mark from the parking lot conversation with Frank.

He texts me on a Sunday morning. The same text he sends every week:

*Dinner Sunday? Your mom's making pot roast.*

I look at the phone. This text. This ordinary, routine, unremarkable text. Every Sunday for years. The same question. The same answer.

In the first life, I never got these texts. By twenty, I'd changed my number three times. Frank didn't have it. Couldn't reach me. Probably tried. Probably called the old numbers and got disconnected tones and eventually stopped trying, because even Frank — even patient, steady, show-up-anyway Frank — has a limit.

But not in this life. In this life, the text comes every Sunday. And every Sunday, I answer.

I type back: *I'll be there.*

Three words. The easiest three words in the world. The hardest three words in another life.

---

The phone buzzes again. Elena, from the kitchen: *Also tell your dad I'm bringing that eggplant dish he pretends to hate.*

I smile. Type back: *He'll complain.*

Elena: *And eat three servings.*

Elena: *That man is a fraud.*

Elena: *A lovable fraud.*

I put the phone down. Hazard is beside me on the bed — fully on the bed now, all pretense of floor-sleeping abandoned. His muzzle is gray. His eyes are cloudy on the left side. He's old. He won't be here forever.

But he's here now. Warm and heavy and breathing against my hip.

---

I get up. Shower. Dress. Normal Sunday things.

In the kitchen, Elena is making the eggplant dish. The kitchen smells like garlic and olive oil. She's humming something — off-key, like Frank, though she'd deny the comparison.

"Coffee's made," she says.

"Thanks."

I pour a cup. Stand at the counter. Watch her cook.

"Stop watching me cook."

"Can't help it."

"You're creepy."

"Noted."

She points the spatula at me. "Go do something useful. Walk the dog."

"The dog's asleep."

"Then sit and drink your coffee and stop making that face."

"What face?"

"The grateful face. The 'I can't believe this is my life' face. You make it every Sunday morning."

"Do I?"

"Every single one." She turns back to the stove. "It's not a bad face. It's just—" She pauses. Stirs. "I see you, Nate. Every time."

I drink my coffee. The morning light comes in through the window over the sink. The eggplant sizzles. Hazard wanders in, drawn by the smell of garlic, and parks himself at Elena's feet with surgical precision.

"Don't give him any," I say.

"I would never."

She absolutely will.

---

Afternoon. We drive to Frank and Diane's. The eggplant in a casserole dish on Elena's lap. Hazard in the back seat, nose pressed to the window.

The house looks the same as it always has. White siding. Blue shutters. The porch swing Frank installed twenty years ago. The garden in the side yard — Diane's, immaculate, even now. Especially now. She's been planting more this year. Root vegetables. Things that store.

She knows. She's preparing, in her way.

Frank is on the porch. Coffee cup. Flannel jacket even though it's April and warming up. He raises a hand as we pull in.

"Eggplant?" he says as we walk up.

"You'll love it," Elena says.

"I'll tolerate it."

"Same thing."

He stands. Holds the door. Hazard goes in first — knows the layout, goes straight to the kitchen where the treats are.

Inside: warmth. The smell of pot roast. Diane at the stove, apron on, radio playing low.

"Sophie's running late," Diane says. "David's working."

"Just us then," Frank says. Settles into his chair at the head of the table.

The four of us. Plus Hazard, who counts.

We eat. We talk. The pot roast is perfect — it's always perfect. Frank complains about the eggplant and eats two helpings. Diane talks about her garden. Elena talks about the veterinary network. Frank asks questions — real ones, engaged ones. He understands now. He understands all of it. And he's not afraid.

Or if he is, he doesn't show it. Because that's Frank. Because facing what's coming is no different from facing a burst pipe in a frozen basement at 3 AM — you assess, you plan, you fix what you can, and you don't waste time on what you can't.

After dinner, I help with dishes. Same as always. Frank washes. I dry.

"The network test," he says. Low. Private. "Sophie said it went well."

"Ninety-six percent."

"And the four percent?"

"Fixed."

He nods. Hands me a plate. "Good."

Silence. Water running. The clink of dishes.

"You scared?" he asks.

I consider lying. Don't.

"Yeah."

"Me too." He scrubs a pot. "But. We're ready as we can be."

"Yeah."

"And the people are good."

"They are."

"Then—" He rinses the pot. Sets it on the rack. "Then we do what we do. Show up. Handle it."

"Yeah."

He drains the sink. Dries his hands. Looks at me.

"You know what you did, right?"

"What do you mean?"

"All of this." He gestures — small, encompassing. The kitchen. The house. The family in the next room. The network beyond. "You could have done anything with what you knew. And you built a family. Built a team. Built a thing that might save people." He puts his hand on my shoulder. Solid. Heavy. "That's not nothing, bud."

"I know."

"I mean it." He squeezes once. Lets go. "Whatever happens next — whenever they get here — you did what you could. And that's enough."

I put the pot away. Close the cabinet.

"Thanks, Dad."

He nods. Walks to the living room. The La-Z-Boy. The television. The documentary — tonight it's about deep-sea creatures, glowing things in the dark.

I stand in the kitchen doorway and watch my family. Diane on the couch, knitting something. Elena cross-legged on the floor, scratching Hazard's belly. Frank in his chair, glasses on, watching luminescent fish drift through black water.

This is what I came back for. Not the objects in the sky. Not the shelters or the seed vaults or the satellite links. This room. These people. This moment.

In fourteen months, the sky changes. In fourteen months, everything I've built gets tested.

But tonight, there's pot roast in my stomach and my father is alive and my family is whole and the dog is getting his belly scratched and the objects are far away and abstract and the evening is warm and the spring peepers are singing outside and everything — for one more night, for this night — everything is okay.

Anyway. The point is—

Not yet. Soon. But not yet.

The point is: I'll be there.
## Chapter 24: June Fourteenth

I wake at 4:11 AM on the morning of my thirty-fifth birthday and I know before I open my eyes.

Not because of a sound. Not because of a text or an alarm or any of the monitoring systems that Chen spent three years calibrating. I know because the air is different. There's a pressure behind my sinuses that wasn't there when I fell asleep — subtle, the way barometric changes are subtle, the way the first tremor before an earthquake is subtle. Something that the body registers before the mind finds language for it.

I'm lying in the dark with Elena beside me. Her breathing is slow and even. Her hand is on my chest, fingers curled loosely against the fabric of my t-shirt. The room smells like her shampoo and the lavender sachet she keeps in the top dresser drawer. Normal things. Small things. Things that exist in the last few hours before everything changes.

I don't move. I stay there for sixty seconds — I count them — and I let myself have this. The weight of her hand. The sound of her breathing. Hazard's tags jingling faintly as he shifts at the foot of the bed, dreaming about something that makes his legs twitch. The absolute stillness of a house at four in the morning when no one else is awake and the world hasn't started yet.

Then I reach for my phone.

Three messages from Chen. The first at 3:47 AM: *Particulate density spike. Twelve sigma above baseline. Not a drill.* The second at 3:52: *Calling NEORAN alert cascade in 30 minutes. Confirm receipt.* The third at 4:03: *Nate. It's today.*

I sit up. Elena stirs.

"Nate?"

"It's time," I say.

She's quiet for three seconds. Then she sits up too. No questions. No panic. Just the rustle of sheets, the click of the bedside lamp. She's already reaching for the go-bag under the bed — the one we packed together six months ago, the one she checks every two weeks without being asked.

"I'll get Hazard's crate," she says. "You call Frank."

I nod. She's moving — quiet, purposeful, no wasted motion. I watch her for two seconds in the lamplight. Brown hair tangled from sleep. Paint still under her fingernails from last night. A woman who decided to believe me and then decided to stay and then decided to be ready, and who is now proving all three of those decisions correct in the space of thirty seconds.

I call Frank.

It rings four times. Five. My father has never been a fast riser and his phone lives on the dresser across the room because he read somewhere that the radiation is bad for you. This is a man who worked with welding torches for thirty years without safety glasses until 2015 but draws the line at cell phone radiation. The sixth ring.

"Yeah." His voice. Graveled with sleep but alert underneath. Frank Callahan doesn't do groggy. He does asleep and he does awake and the transition between them takes about one and a half seconds.

"Dad. It's Nate."

"I know who it is. It's four in the goddamn morning."

"Dad. It's today."

Silence. Two seconds. Three.

"Today today."

"Yes, sir."

I hear him breathe. One long breath in, one long breath out. I hear the bedsprings creak as he sits up. I hear, distantly, Diane's voice — a murmur, a question.

"We'll be there," Frank says. "Give us forty minutes."

"The gymnasium. Not the mill — the gymnasium at Jefferson Elementary. That's where I'll be."

"Forty minutes." He hangs up.

I stare at the phone. My hand is steady. I notice this the way you notice that the sky is still dark outside the window — as a fact, without commentary. Eighteen years of preparation. Eighteen years of knowing this morning would come. And now that it's here, my hand is steady, and I don't know what that means about me.

---

The drive to the gymnasium takes eleven minutes. Elena is in the passenger seat. Hazard is in the back, nose pressed to the window, sensing something in the air that he can't name but doesn't like. His whining is low and continuous, a sound I've never heard him make before. The streets are empty. It's 4:38 AM and Cedar Falls is still asleep, the houses dark, the stoplights cycling through their colors for no one.

The sky is wrong. Not gray yet — not the flat, featureless gray I remember from the first timeline. But the stars are gone. The predawn sky should be clear tonight — I checked the forecast yesterday out of habit, the way I've checked it every day for eighteen years — and instead there's a haze. High up. Barely visible unless you know what you're looking for. A faint particulate shimmer that eats the starlight.

I know what's up there. I've known for eighteen years. But knowing a thing and seeing it begin are different in a way that lives in the body, not the mind. My stomach is tight. My jaw aches. I realize I'm clenching my teeth and I force myself to stop.

"The stars are gone," Elena says. She's looking out her window, face tilted up.

"Yeah."

"How long until it's visible? The gray?"

"Last time, the sky was fully gray by sunrise. Maybe a little after."

"So people will wake up and it'll be — done. Already changed."

"Yes."

She reaches across the console and puts her hand on my leg. Doesn't say anything. Just the weight of her hand. I cover it with mine and we drive the rest of the way in silence.

---

The gymnasium at Jefferson Elementary is a building I've spent four years preparing. From the outside, it still looks like a school gym — yellow brick, high windows with wire mesh, the kind of building that every small city in the Midwest has forty of. But the basement has been reinforced. The water recycling system went live in March. The hydroponic grow rooms in the converted locker rooms have been producing greens for twelve weeks. The air filtration can run forty-eight hours on the backup generators, longer on the solar array we installed on the roof last summer.

It looks like a school gym. It's a lifeboat.

Marcus is already there when I pull in. His truck is parked at the loading dock, tailgate down, and he's carrying plastic bins of medical supplies through the service entrance. He sees my headlights and straightens up — six-three, broad-shouldered, his face unreadable in the dark.

"Chen's cascade went out at 4:15," he says as I get out. "I was here by 4:20. Sophie's on her way — she's picking up the comms equipment from the storage unit."

"Good. How's the building?"

"Structure's solid. I did the walk-through last week. Roof's holding, foundation's dry, no cracks in the reinforcement. We're good." He hesitates. "Nate."

"Yeah."

"Today."

"Today."

He nods. Once. The same nod he's given me for fifteen years — since we were twenty, since I told him the truth in his dorm room and he decided to believe me, since we rebuilt a friendship on the foundation of an impossible story that kept proving itself right. The nod that says: *I'm here. We're doing this. What's next.*

"Let's get the lights on," I say.

---

By 5:30 AM, the gymnasium is operational. The overhead fluorescents cast their flat institutional light over a space that has been transformed: sleeping areas along the east wall, marked with blue tape on the hardwood floor. Medical triage near the locker room entrance — three folding tables, supply bins, a partition screen Elena found at a thrift store. Communications station at the scorer's table — Sophie's domain, three laptops and a satellite uplink and a marine radio that can reach the other NEORAN nodes within two hundred miles. Food prep in the old concession area. Water station by the south doors.

We've drilled this. Four times in the last year, full-scale exercises with volunteers. Each time it took thirty minutes to get the space configured. Tonight it takes nineteen. Nobody is pretending anymore.

Elena is at medical, laying out supplies with the focused precision of someone who took a wilderness EMT course fourteen months ago specifically for this morning. She didn't have to do that. She chose to. The way she chose all of it — chose to read my files, chose to believe, chose to stay, chose to learn tourniquet application and IV placement and wound debridement because the woman she fell in love with was a man who knew the world was ending and she decided that if the world was ending then she was going to be useful when it did.

Sophie arrives at 5:42. She comes through the main doors carrying two duffel bags of communications gear, her hair pulled back in a ponytail, her face set in the expression I've come to recognize as her version of Frank's flat competence — the look that says *there will be time for feelings later; right now there is work.*

"Satellite link is up in the truck," she says, dropping the bags at the scorer's table. "I'll have the base station running in ten minutes. Chen's already on the network — she pinged me from the university lab."

"Good. What about the other nodes?"

"Des Moines confirmed active. Omaha confirmed. Minneapolis is coming online now. Kansas City hasn't responded yet but their activation window isn't until 5:45 Central, so give them a minute."

She's already unpacking equipment, hands moving with the specific efficiency of someone who has done this exact sequence of actions in practice and is now doing it for real and finding, with some satisfaction, that the practice holds. My sister. Four years ago she was a social worker who'd never touched a satellite radio. Now she's running communications for a regional survival network because her brother asked her to learn and she said yes.

I think about the version of Sophie who wouldn't look at me at Dad's funeral. The version who said *Nate, I'm scared of you* over the phone when I was twenty-six and drunk. The distance between that version and this one — the woman unpacking radios at 5:42 AM with steady hands — is the distance I traveled. Not alone. Never alone. But I'm the one who had to walk it first, before anyone else would follow.

---

6:14 AM. Sunrise.

Except there is no sunrise. The sky goes gray.

Not gradually. Not the slow fade I've been watching since 4 AM. At 6:14 — the exact minute, the precise timestamp that has lived in my memory for eighteen years — the haze that has been building in the upper atmosphere reaches a density that blocks direct sunlight. The quality of light coming through the gymnasium's high windows shifts. One moment it's the thin blue of predawn. The next it's flat. Even. Gray. The same gray I saw from my apartment window in the first timeline, the morning I woke up hungover and thirty-five and already dead in every way that mattered.

I'm standing in the middle of the gymnasium floor. Around me, six volunteers are setting up the last of the sleeping cots. Sophie is at her station, headphones on. Marcus is in the basement checking the water system. Elena is organizing medication into labeled bins.

Nobody screams. Nobody panics. The people in this room — the eight of us, the early crew, the ones who knew what was coming — we just look up. We look at the windows. We see the gray. And we keep working.

It's the people who arrive later who will be afraid. The ones who wake up to a changed sky and no explanation. The ones whose phones will stop working when the cell towers overload. The ones who will remember this morning for the rest of their lives as the morning the world broke.

But in this gymnasium, at 6:14 AM on June 14, 2026 — my thirty-fifth birthday, the anniversary of my first death — the lights are on and the water system is pressurized and the satellite link is broadcasting and we keep working. Because that is the only sane response to the end of the world. You keep working.

---

By 8 AM, people are arriving.

Not the coordinated arrivals of NEORAN members who got the cascade alert. Those people — forty-three of them in the Cedar Falls node — arrived between 5:30 and 7:00, calm, carrying go-bags, knowing what to do. These are the others. The neighbors. The scared ones. The ones who woke up to gray sky and dead lawns and phones that don't connect and drove to the one place in town they remembered hearing about — the community emergency shelter at Jefferson Elementary, the one that nice nonprofit organized, the one that sent flyers last spring.

They come in waves. A family of four with a toddler in a car seat, the father's face white, the mother carrying a diaper bag and a gallon of water. An elderly couple — the Hendersons, I know them from the hardware store — with a cat in a carrier and a plastic bin of canned goods. Three college-age kids from UNI, still in pajama pants, clutching their phones like talismans even though the screens show no signal.

I'm at the door. I don't have to be — we have a check-in process, volunteers assigned to intake. But I'm at the door because this is the thing I built and these are the people I built it for and I need to see their faces when they come in. I need to see the fear shift to something else when they walk through the doors and see the lights on and the cots set up and Sophie's calm voice on the PA saying *welcome, please check in at the desk to your left, water is available at the south wall.*

The fear doesn't go away. But something else layers on top of it. Relief. Recognition. The particular expression of a person who realizes that someone thought about this before they did. That someone prepared. That the ground under their feet, in this one building, in this one small city in Iowa, is solid.

By 9 AM, we have a hundred and twelve people in the gymnasium.

By 10, a hundred and sixty.

By 10:30, Marcus has done his first structural assessment of the day and confirmed the building is holding fine, and Chen is on satellite link with four other NEORAN nodes reporting similar numbers, and Elena has treated two panic attacks and one sprained ankle from someone tripping on the curb in the parking lot, and Sophie has established communication with the county emergency management office which is, predictably, overwhelmed and grateful to hear that we exist.

And the grass outside is dying.

I can see it through the propped-open doors when new people arrive. The lawn in front of the school — the one the janitor mowed last Thursday, the one with the dandelions growing along the chain-link fence — is browning. Not uniformly. In patches, spreading, the green draining out of the blades the way color drains from a face. The oak tree in the parking strip has leaves that are curling inward. It's June. The leaves should be full and dark green and making shade for the kids who would have been playing here in two weeks when school let out. Instead they're pulling tight, going the color of old paper, drifting down one by one.

I watch this for thirty seconds through the open door. Then I close the door. Not because I'm afraid to see it. Because there are a hundred and sixty people in this building who don't need to watch the world die in real time. Not yet. They'll see it soon enough.

---

Noon. The sky has not changed. The gray is constant — no variation, no movement, no clouds distinguishable within it. Just flat gray from horizon to horizon, the same dead light falling everywhere, casting no shadows because there's no directionality to it. Like being inside a lightbox. Like the sun has been replaced with a fluorescent panel the size of the sky.

I'm in the old coach's office that we've converted to a command center — a desk, a laptop connected to Chen's monitoring network, a whiteboard with the day's status written in Sophie's neat print. Marcus is here. Elena. Sophie, taking a break from comms to eat a protein bar.

Chen's face is on the laptop screen, pixelated slightly by the satellite connection. Behind her, the university lab is lit by the same flat gray coming through the windows. Boltzmann is asleep on a stack of papers beside her keyboard. She looks tired. She looks focused. She looks like a woman who has been right about something terrible for three years and is now watching her data become reality.

"Twelve nodes reporting nominal across the network," she says. "Omaha has four hundred people. Des Moines is approaching capacity — they're opening the secondary site. Minneapolis is online but having generator issues. Kansas City finally responded. They're operational."

"How many people total?" I ask. "Across all nodes."

"Approximately four thousand, as of last count. Growing."

Four thousand. Out of three hundred and thirty million. I don't do the math because the math is devastating. But four thousand people are in buildings with filtered air and clean water and hydroponic food production and communication networks and medical supplies, because eighteen years ago a man woke up in his childhood bedroom with knowledge of the future and decided to do something with it other than drink himself to death.

Four thousand. It's not enough. It was never going to be enough. Elena told me that, years ago, in her apartment with paint on her hands and my files spread across the kitchen table. *Three hundred and thirty million in this country. Then scale it.* We scaled it. We scaled it as far as money and time and volunteers and permits and physics allowed. And it's still not enough, and it was never going to be, and the only choice was between not enough and nothing.

"The vessels are visible now," Chen says. "Naked eye. Southern horizon."

I go outside.

---

The parking lot is half-full — people's cars, abandoned wherever they stopped. The air is different than it was at 4 AM. Not the pressure I felt then — something else now. A flatness. The smell of nothing. The same subtraction of scent I remember from the first timeline, when the air in my apartment complex seemed to have been cleaned of everything that made it air and replaced with something that was breathable but empty.

I look south.

They're there. Four — no. I count. Seven. Seven shapes on the southern horizon, above the tree line, above the buildings. They're vast. The word that comes to mind is *platform* — flat, broad, featureless shapes that hang in the gray sky with no visible means of support. No lights. No sound. No movement that I can detect. They are simply there, the way a mesa is there, the way a mountain is there — geological in their indifference. As if they have always been part of the sky and we are only now noticing.

They're not warships. I know this because I saw what they did in the first timeline. They don't attack. They don't threaten. They harvest. The way a combine harvester doesn't threaten wheat. The wheat is irrelevant to the machine except as material. The machine isn't hostile. The machine isn't friendly. The machine is a machine.

The Kessari — the name Chen gave them three years ago, from a Sanskrit word meaning "that which extracts" — are not here for us. We are not the point. The planet is the point. The biomass is the point. We are incidental to the harvest the way mice are incidental to a wheat field being cut.

I stand in the parking lot and I look at them and I feel — less than I expected to feel. In the first timeline, I never saw them clearly. I was dead before they arrived in force. Now I'm seeing them for the first time with sober eyes and a clear mind and I feel not terror but a deep, quiet sadness. The sadness of confirmation. The sadness of having been right for eighteen years and wishing, more than anything, that I had been wrong.

I go back inside. There are a hundred and eighty people in the gymnasium now. They need me in here more than the sky needs me to look at it.

---

1:47 PM. A commotion at the main entrance. I'm across the floor helping Marcus anchor a partition wall and I hear the voices — one of them raised, aggressive, frightened. I cross the gymnasium in long strides.

Derek Holt is at the door. Two of our intake volunteers are between him and the main floor. He's not trying to push past them — he's standing there, coat on, bag over one shoulder, looking around the gymnasium with an expression I can't immediately read. Not panic. Not relief.

Recognition.

He sees me. I see him. The volunteers look between us.

"Let him in," I say.

Derek walks toward me. He's older than the last time I saw him — when was that? Six years ago? Seven? A chance encounter at a gas station on the interstate. He'd been driving somewhere. I'd been driving somewhere. We'd nodded at each other across the pumps the way men do when they used to be friends and aren't anymore but aren't enemies either. The neutral nod of elapsed time.

He's thinner now. His hair is going gray at the temples. He's wearing a canvas jacket and work boots and he looks like a man who drove here from somewhere else because he remembered something he'd heard years ago and didn't believe it then but believes it now because the sky is gray and the grass is dead and the things on the horizon are not going away.

"Nate." He stops in front of me. Close enough that I can see the broken capillaries along his nose, the lines around his eyes. "Nate Callahan."

"Derek."

"You knew." Not a question. A statement. Flat. His eyes move across the gymnasium — the cots, the medical station, the communications array, the organized efficiency of a place that was prepared for this exact morning. "You knew. For eighteen years."

"Yes."

"And you spent that time — what? Making amends? Playing house?"

I look at him. Derek Holt, who I drank with in college. Who I ghosted when I got sober. Who heard rumors about my weird survivalist nonprofit and made jokes about it at parties, probably. Who is standing in my shelter now because the world is ending and my shelter is the only building in Cedar Falls with lights on and water running and someone at the door saying *welcome.*

"Yes," I say.

"You could have been a king." His voice is quiet now. Not angry. Something else — bewildered, maybe. The bewilderment of a man looking at a choice he doesn't understand. "If you knew all this was coming — you could have owned everything. You could have been on top when it all fell apart."

"I was," I say. "In the first version. It wasn't worth it."

He stares at me. Five seconds. Ten. I don't know what he sees in my face. I don't know what the answer looks like from the outside — whether it looks like wisdom or insanity or the specific calm of a man who has been doing the math on this trade for two decades and is certain of the result.

"Find a cot," I say. "East wall. There's water at the south station. We'll have food at six."

He nods. Slowly. Then he walks toward the east wall. Sets his bag on a cot. Sits down. Looks at his hands.

I watch him for a moment. Then I turn back to the floor. There are things to do.

---

3:15 PM. Frank's truck.

I hear it before I see it — the rumble of the F-150's engine, the one that's needed a new muffler since 2019, the one Frank keeps saying he'll fix next weekend and never does. I'm at the south doors checking the water station when the sound registers, and something in my chest goes tight in a way that has nothing to do with the Kessari.

I go to the main entrance. Frank's truck is pulling into the lot — slow, careful, the way he drives when the roads are icy even though the roads aren't icy, because Frank Callahan drives the same way in every condition: steady, unhurried, hands at ten and two. The truck bed is full. I can see blankets. Cases of canned goods — beans, soup, vegetables. A cooler. A five-gallon jug of water. Two folding chairs.

He parks. Diane gets out of the passenger side first — my mother, sixty-three, her face pale but composed, wearing the puffy vest she bought at Costco three years ago and carrying a canvas tote bag. Frank gets out the driver's side. Flannel shirt. Work boots. Sixty-six years old, a stent in his left anterior descending artery that was placed eighteen months ago, and he's lifting a case of Campbell's soup from the truck bed like it weighs nothing.

Nobody told him to bring supplies. Nobody sent him a list. He just — brought them. Because there's a crisis and his son is running a shelter and Frank Callahan shows up to crises with what he has, and what he has is a truck full of blankets and canned goods and forty years of showing up.

I walk to the truck.

"Dad."

He sets the soup on the tailgate. Looks at me. His face does the thing it always does — the minimal assessment, the quick scan for damage. He's been doing this since I was seventeen and came downstairs a different person and he couldn't tell why but he knew something had changed. He's been doing it for eighteen years and it still takes him less than two seconds.

"You look like hell," he says.

"Yeah. Busy morning."

"Looks like." He pulls another case from the bed — green beans, store brand. "Where do you want this stuff?"

"Inside. Food prep is in the old concession area."

"Diane's got blankets in the back seat. Twenty — the good ones, the wool ones she keeps in the hall closet."

I help him unload. We don't talk about the sky. We don't talk about the things on the horizon. We carry soup and blankets and a five-gallon jug of water through the main doors of a gymnasium that is holding two hundred people now, and the act of carrying is its own language. Frank's language. The language I spent eighteen years learning to speak.

Inside, Diane has already found the families with small children. She's distributing blankets, talking in the low, firm voice she uses when things are serious — the voice from the hospital when Frank had his stent placed, the voice from the phone calls after Dad's first heart attack. Not soothing, exactly. Competent. The voice of a woman who raised two children and worked thirty years as a dental hygienist and knows that what people need in a crisis isn't comfort. It's someone who appears to know what they're doing.

Frank carries the last case in, sets it in the concession area, and then — without being asked, without looking for direction — starts walking the floor. Blankets over his arm. Handing them out. Nodding at people. Not talking much. Just present. Visible. A sixty-six-year-old man in a flannel shirt distributing blankets in a gymnasium because that's what there is to do and he's here and it needs doing.

I stand by the door and I watch him for a minute. I watch my father move through a room full of frightened people and make each one of them a little less frightened, not through words or reassurance but through the sheer fact of his presence. Through the unshakeable ordinariness of a man handing out blankets. Through the message that this act transmits without language: *someone is here. Someone knows what to do. You are not alone in this.*

I think about the version of this day where I never came back. The version where I stayed dead in my apartment at thirty-five, unmade by the white light with nothing to show for my life except empty bottles and an eviction notice. The version where Frank died of his heart attack at sixty-three with a drunk son who couldn't iron a shirt for the funeral. The version where this gymnasium is just a gymnasium, dark and locked, and the people outside have nowhere to go.

I think about this for exactly five seconds. Then I stop, because there is no room in this day for the luxury of reflection. There are two hundred and fourteen people in this building and the number is growing and the sky is gray and will stay gray and there is work to do.

---
